On a (terminally connected, pro-etale) factorization of geometric morphisms
Olivia Caramello, Axel Osmond

Abstract
We extend the classical (connected, etale) factorization of locally connected geometric morphisms into a (terminally connected, pro-etale) factorization for all geometric morphisms between Grothendieck topoi. We discuss properties of both classes of morphisms, particularly the relation between pro-etale geometric morphisms and the category of global elements of their inverse image; we also discuss their stability properties as well as some fibrational aspects.



Introduction

A classical result of topos theory states that any locally connected geometric morphism can be factored in a unique way as a connected geometric morphism followed by the etale geometric morphism over its object of connected components. There are two possible generalizations of this result. On one hand, keeping connected morphisms as the left class gives a
(connected, disconnected)
factorization, see
[
8
]
and
[
9
]
(where the left class is also called
algebraic
. But there is another possible generalization.

It was proved by the first author in
[
2
]
that essential geometric morphisms also admit a canonical factorization through the essential image of the terminal object – which still mimics the behavior of the functor of connected components to some degree. In general the left part of this factorization is no longer connected; however, it still is
terminally connected
. In the essential world, this condition states that the essential image preserves the terminal object – which still can be read as a “global” form of connectedness; but a more general formulation of this condition, which makes sense for any geometric morphism, is that the inverse image lifts uniquely global elements – which amounts in some sense as being connected from the point of view of the terminal object. Terminally connected geometric morphisms happen to be exactly those that are left orthogonal to etale geometric morphisms, which suggests they form the left class of a factorization system for all geometric morphisms.

However, extending the (terminally connected, etale) factorization beyond essential geometric morphisms requires to relax in turn the etaleness condition of the right class: we have to consider
cofiltered bilimits
of etale geometric morphisms. Indeed, in full generality a geometric morphism may lack an essential image part displaying connected components; yet, its inverse image part, as a lex functor, actually always possesses a
left pro-adjoint
. In some sense, although the connected components of an arbitrary geometric morphism may not be indexed by an discrete set internal to the codomain topos, they will nevertheless form a pro-discrete internal locale.

Factorizing a geometric morphism through the etale geometric morphism at a given object of its codomain amounts to providing a global element of the inverse image of this given object; similarly factorizations through pro-etale geometric morphisms correspond to cofiltered diagrams of such global elements. In particular, there is a best such factorization through the pro-etale morphism indexed by the cofiltered category of all global elements of the inverse image part; although this category is large, an accessibility argument shows that it admits a small initial subcategory, which ensures the bilimit to be well defined. In particular, any pro-etale geometric morphism can be reindexed by the category of elements of its own inverse image, which provides a canonical presentation.

If now one factorizes a geometric morphism through the pro-etale morphism indexed by all the possible global elements of the inverse image, the residual left part is then always terminally connected, for in some sense all global elements are displayed in a faithful way in the cofiltered bilimit. This begets a
(terminally connected, pro-etale)
factorization for all geometric morphisms. Moreover, while it was known that locally connected and terminally connected geometric morphisms are automatically connected, we dually have that essential pro-etale geometric morphisms are actually etale as their cofiltered diagram of presentation admits an initial object, namely the unit of the adjunction between the inverse image and the essential image. Hence this factorization restricts correctly to the known ones. A peculiar manifestation of this factorization is already known in the instance of the so-called
Grothendieck-Verdier localization
(or at least, a generalization of it given in
[
7
]
), which is the (terminally connected, pro-etale) factorization at a point, and is reminiscent of the construction of the
germ
at this point, that is, the cofiltered intersections of its neighborhoods – except that now one may consider etale neighborhoods rather than merely open ones.

Remarkably, this factorization allows for a canonical factorization of lax cells. In general 2-dimensional factorization systems do not come with a canonical way to relate the factorizations of two 1-cells related by a 2-cell; equivalently, the orthogonality condition for (pseudo)squares does not extend to either lax or oplax squares. But in this very case, it happens to be so. Additionally, terminally connected morphisms enjoy a special stability property along bicomma squares.

Those properties are reminiscent of those of one of the
comprehensive factorization systems
for functors – the
(initial, discrete opfibration)
factorization, to which it is indeed related in several ways. First, in
[
2
]
the factorization of essential morphisms involved an analog of this comprehensive factorization for comorphisms of sites – although it is unclear whether this can generalize here. Secondly, it can be shown that pro-etale morphisms are internal discrete opfibration, a property preserved at the level of points. Although it is not true that terminally connected morphisms induce initial functors between categories of points, we will see they still behave in some sense as “topologically initial” morphisms, for one can show that the comma topos of a terminally connected geometric morphism over a point is a connected topos, which is a form of initialness.

This paper is articulated as follows: we first recall generalities about the (connected, etale) factorization and introduce terminally connected morphisms. In the second section, after a digression on cofiltered bilimits of Grothendieck topoi, we introduce pro-etale geometric morphisms and discuss their canonical presentation through global elements. We then show the main theorem of this paper, the promised factorization – and also its laxness aspects. Next we establish a more intrinsic characterization of pro-etale geometric morphisms as those that generate their domain through fibers of global elements. We dually investigate properties of terminally connected geometric morphisms, in particular their relation with cardinals, and discuss the quite delicate matter of their site presentation. We end with some stability properties, and a discussion on their possible relation with initial functors.

1

(Connected, etale) factorization and its generalization

In this section, we recall established facts about the connected-etale factorization for locally connected geometric morphisms and its more recent generalization to essential geometric morphisms provided in
[
2
]
; we also give a precise account of the relation between etale geometric morphisms and global elements, which shall play a key role throughout this paper.

In the following, we work in the 2-category
{\bf GTop}
of Grothendieck topoi, geometric morphisms and geometric transformations. We shall denote topoi as
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}
… and geometric as
f,g
… We shall denote as
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
the pseudoslice of
{\bf GTop}
over
\mathcal{E}
.

1.1
Orthogonality and factorization for geometric morphisms

In this subsection, we first recall some generalities on 2-dimensional orthogonality structures and factorization systems. We then discuss some of their particularities in
{\bf GTop}
. In most sources, the different (2-dimensional) factorization systems of
{\bf GTop}
are manipulated as ordinary factorization systems of the underlying category, which is indeed innocuous in practice: for this reason, the hurry reader who is not interested in the 2-dimensional details of those factorizations systems may safely skip those prerequisites. However, because most of the structure of
{\bf GTop}
is actually bicategorical and universal constructions in geometric contexts are often weak rather than strict, we think preferable to state exactly the bicategorical versions of orthogonality and factorization, especially considering that our factorization system shall enjoy special additional 2-dimensional properties. The main references for strict 2-dimensional factorization systems are
[
5
]
, and
[
11
]
for the bicategorical ones.

Let us fix a 2-category
\mathcal{K}
. Denote as
2
the category consisting of a single arrow. We denote as
{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{K}]
the 2-category of 2-functors
f:2\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
, pseudonatural transformations
\alpha:f\rightarrow g
, and natural modifications between those. A close examination shows that objects of this 2-categories are exactly 1-cells of
\mathcal{K}
. For
f:A\rightarrow B
and
g:C\rightarrow D
two 1-cells in
\mathcal{K}
, a morphism
f\rightarrow g
in
{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{K}]
consists exactly of a
pseudocommutative square
, or
pseudosquare
from
f
to
g
is a 2-functor the data of a triple
(u,v,\alpha):f\Rightarrow g
with
\alpha:gu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\Rightarrow}}vf
an invertible 2-cell as below,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

Now, a 2-cell corresponds to a morphism of pseudosquares
(\phi,\psi):(u,v,\alpha)\Rightarrow(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
, which is the data of
\phi:u\Rightarrow u^{\prime}
and
\psi:v\Rightarrow v^{\prime}
such that
\alpha^{\prime}g^{*}\phi=\psi^{*}f\alpha
as depicted below

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

One may recognize that the category of pseudosquares from
f
to
g
as the pseudopullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{K}](f,g)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}[A,C]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}[B,D]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}[A,D]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}[f,D]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}[A,g]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{f,g}\atop\simeq}

On the other hand, for a pair of maps
f:A\rightarrow B
and
g:C\rightarrow D
in a 2-category
\mathcal{K}
, precomposition by
f
and postcomposition by
g
induce altogether a strictly commutative square in
{\bf Cat}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}[B,C]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}[A,C]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}[B,D]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}[A,D]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}[f,D]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}[A,g]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}[B,g]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}[f,C]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Definition 1.1.1
.

We say
f
and
g
are respectively
left and right bi-orthogonal to each other
, and denote as
f\perp g
, if one has an equivalence of categories:

\mathcal{K}[B,C]\simeq{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{K}](f,g)

This condition exactly means that for any pseudosquare
(u,v,\alpha)
there is an arrow
s_{\alpha}:B\rightarrow C
, unique up to a unique invertible 2-cell, together with a pair of invertible 2-cells
(\lambda_{\alpha},\rho_{\alpha})
as depicted below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\alpha}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}\atop\simeq}

while for a morphism of pseudosquares
(\phi,\psi):(u,v,\alpha)\Rightarrow(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
there exists a unique 2-cell
\delta_{(\phi,\psi)}:s_{\alpha}\Rightarrow s_{\alpha^{\prime}}
such that

\alpha^{\prime}\phi=\sigma^{*}f\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}g^{*}\sigma=\psi\alpha
as depicted below:

Definition 1.1.2
.

A
bi-orthogonality structure
on a 2-category
\mathcal{K}
consists of a pair of classes of 1-cells
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
with
\mathcal{L}=\,^{\perp}\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{L}^{\perp}
.

Proposition 1.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
is a 2-category endowed with a bi-orthogonality structure
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
. Then the left and right classes enjoy the following properties :

-

\mathcal{L}
is closed under composition

-

\mathcal{L}
is closed under invertible 2-cell

-

\mathcal{L}
contains all equivalences

-

\mathcal{L}
is right-pseudocancellative
: for any invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

with
l_{1},\;l_{2}
in
\mathcal{L}
, then
f
also is in
\mathcal{L}

-

\mathcal{L}
is closed under weighted bicolimits in
{\bf ps}[{2,\mathcal{K}}]

-

\mathcal{L}
is closed under bipushout along arbitrary maps

-

\mathcal{R}
is closed under composition

-

\mathcal{R}
is closed under invertible 2-cell

-

\mathcal{R}
contains all equivalences

-

\mathcal{R}
is left-pseudocancellative
: for any invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

with
r_{1},\;r_{2}
in
\mathcal{R}
, then
f
also is in
\mathcal{R}

-

\mathcal{R}
is closed under weighted bilimits in
{\bf ps}[{2,\mathcal{K}}]

-

\mathcal{R}
is closed under bipullback along arbitrary maps

Lemma 1.1.4
.

In a bi-orthogonality structure one automatically has the following, then
\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{R}
consists exactly of all equivalences.

Definition 1.1.5
.

A
bifactorization system
on
\mathcal{K}
is the data of a bi-orthogonality structure
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
such that for any
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathcal{K}
, there exists a pair
(l_{f},r_{f})
with
l_{f}\in\mathcal{L}
and
r_{f}\in\mathcal{R}
equipped with an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{f}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{f}}

Remark 1.1.6
.

By the orthogonality of left and right maps, any such factorization shall be universal in the sense that, for any other factorization
\alpha:rl\simeq f
with
l_{f}\in\mathcal{L}
and
r_{f}\in\mathcal{R}
, there exist a unique equivalence
i
and a pair of invertible 2-cells
\lambda:il\simeq l_{f}
and
\rho:r\simeq r_{f}i
such that
\alpha=\alpha_{f}\rho^{*}lr^{*}\lambda
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\atop\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{f}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

1.1.7
.

The 2-category
{\bf GTop}
of Grothendieck topoi and geometric morphisms admits several well known orthogonal factorization systems:

-

the (surjection, embedding) factorization;

-

the (hyperconnected, localic) factorization;

-

the (dense, closed) factorization;

-

the (pure, entire) factorization…

One can show that all those factorization systems are actually bifactorization systems, with the orthogonality property extending without problem to pseudosquares. Now, recall that geometric morphisms can be read in two ways: in the way of the direct image, they convey geometric information, while in the way of the inverse image, they convey algebraic information. Hence, there are always two ways to describe the dual properties of left and right maps in a factorization system for geometric morphisms:

-

from a geometric point of view, left maps are connected in some way; right maps are truncated in some way;

-

from an algebraic point of view, inverse image parts of right maps are generating under some operations, while inverse image parts of left maps are closed under those operations.

In this work, we are going to exhibit a factorization system where both those complementary aspects are particularly manifest. It shall arise as a generalization of a conceptually important factorization system that however only holds for restricted classes of geometric morphisms, the
(connected, etale)
factorization of
locally connected
geometric morphisms. The remaining of this section shall be devoted to the exposition of this factorization system.

1.2
Etale geometric morphisms and (connected, etale) factorization

It is a fondamental result of topos theory that for any topos
\mathcal{E}
and any object
E
in
\mathcal{E}
the slice
\mathcal{E}/E
is a topos and the pullback functor
E^{*}:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}/E
is the inverse image part of a geometric morphism
\pi_{E}:\mathcal{E}/E\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, the
etale geometric morphism at
E
.

Definition 1.2.1
.

A geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is said to be
etale
if there exists an object
E
in
\mathcal{E}
such that one has an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

In the following, we shall denote as
{\bf Et}
the class of etale geometric morphisms, and
{\bf Et}/\mathcal{E}
the 2-category of etale geometric morphisms with fixed codomain
\mathcal{E}
- for which we shall often say,
etale over
\mathcal{E}
.

Remark 1.2.2
.

In practice, we shall innocently consider any etale morphism as exactly being of the form
\mathcal{E}/E\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, ignoring the eventual equivalence through which it is presented.

Any object
E
of a topos
\mathcal{E}
induces an etale geometric morphism
\mathcal{E}/E\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
. Any
u:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
in a topos
\mathcal{E}
induces a canonical invertible 2-cell in
{\bf GTop}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}/u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{u}\atop\simeq}

where the invertible 2-cell
\alpha_{u}
has as inverse image part
\alpha_{u}^{\flat}:u^{*}E^{{}^{\prime}*}\simeq E^{*}
the canonical isomorphism provided by composition of pullbacks at any object
D
of
\mathcal{E}

E\times^{u}_{E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}\times D)\simeq E\times D

Remark 1.2.3
.

The fact that limits are defined only up to a canonical isomorphism makes this a natural isomorphism rather than a pointwise equality. As the 2-cells of
{\bf GTop}
should be taken as being determined by their inverse image component – this shall be our convention – the triangle above really is an invertible 2-cell in
{\bf GTop}
, not a strict 2-cell.

Definition 1.2.4
.

For an object
E
in
\mathcal{E}
, a
global element
of
E
is a morphism
a:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow E
in
\mathcal{E}
, where
1_{\mathcal{E}}
is the terminal object of
\mathcal{E}
. A
morphism of global elements
between two global elements
a:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow E
and
a^{\prime}:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow E^{\prime}
is a morphism
u:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
inducing a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{E}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}

Remark 1.2.5
.

Any global element
a:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow E
is a split-monomorphism with section the terminal map
E\rightarrow 1_{\mathcal{E}}
.

There is a correspondence between global sections of an etale geometric morphisms over
\mathcal{E}
and global elements of the corresponding object. Denote as
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
the pseudoslice of
{\bf GTop}
over
\mathcal{E}
, that is, the slice whose 1-cells
f\rightarrow g
are invertible 2-cells of
{\bf GTop}
of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}

We are going to prove that factorization through etale morphisms are in correspondence with global elements:

Proposition 1.2.6
.

For
E
an object of
\mathcal{E}
, the homcategory
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}[1_{\mathcal{E}},\pi_{E}]
is equivalent to the discrete category given by the set of global elements of
E
:

{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}[1_{\mathcal{E}},\pi_{E}]\simeq\mathcal{E}[1_{\mathcal{E}},E]

Proof.

The process from which one constructs a global element from a factorization is described in
[
6
]
[Proposition B3.2.4(a)] and relies on the formulation of Diaconescu theorem in term of torsors: a geometric morphism
a:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow\pi_{E}
has to be defined by a discrete fibration
X\rightarrow E
from a filtered internal category
X
in
\mathcal{E}
, which must hence be isomorphic to the terminal object
1_{\mathcal{E}}
. Conversely for a global element
a:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow E
one can take the pullback functor along
a
sending
h:D\rightarrow E
to
a^{*}D
; stability of colimits in topoi ensures that this functors is left adjoint, while commutation of limits ensures that it preserves finite limits, hence defines the inverse image part of a geometric morphism. Moreover, cancellation of pullbacks gives a natural isomorphism between
a^{*}E^{*}D
and
D
itself as the latter can be chosen as the pullback as depicted below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\times D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{E}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{E}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{*}D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

∎

Remark 1.2.7
.

Beware that the process above does not beget strictly commuting 2-cells; more generally, one can still classify global elements with geometric morphisms that are only equivalent to etale geometric morphisms through a 2-cell of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}

1.2.8
.

This correspondence generalizes into a correspondence between factorization of geometric morphisms through etale morphisms over
\mathcal{E}
and global elements of their inverse image. Let indeed
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
be a geometric morphism. Suppose one has an invertible 2-cell as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Then we have a factorization of
a
through the base change

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}/f^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{f^{*}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

which also defines a global section of
\pi_{f^{*}E}
, pointing a global element
a:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}E
. Moreover, the inverse image of the geometric morphism
a:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}/E
can be explicitly described in terms of the corresponding global element: it sends any arrow
h:D\rightarrow E
to the pullback in
\mathcal{F}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{*}f^{*}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}f^{*}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Remark 1.2.9
.

This correspondence manifests for each
E
as an isomorphism

{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}[f,\pi_{E}]\simeq\mathcal{F}[1_{\mathcal{F}},f^{*}E]

and those isomorphisms jointly yield an equivalence between the following comma-categories:

1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}\simeq f\downarrow\textbf{Et}/\mathcal{E}

Remark 1.2.10
.

An object
D
in a 2-category
\mathcal{K}
is called
discrete
if for any object
C
the homcategory
\mathcal{K}[C,D]
is equivalent to a discrete category, that is, a set. Here, one can see that etale geometric morphisms are discrete objects in the pseudoslice
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
. From their definition, etale geometric morphisms over
\mathcal{E}
are indexed by objects of
E
; in fact, this correspondence is natural and defines an equivalence of categories

\textbf{Et}/\mathcal{E}\simeq\mathcal{E}

This ensures that one can manipulate
{\bf Et}/\mathcal{E}
as an ordinary category. There is an intuitive reason for this. Objects in a topos are internal sets: but sets are the same as discrete spaces. This intuition is formalized by the fact that etale geometric morphisms over
\mathcal{E}
are
localic
and correspond to
discrete internal locales
in
\mathcal{E}
, which are nothing but objects of
\mathcal{E}
.

Discreteness is the finest kind of truncation: following the geometric interpretation of factorization systems given at
1.1.7
, this suggests that etale geometric morphisms are right orthogonal to a class of morphisms expressing a dual connectedness property. They are indeed right orthogonal to the following class of geometric morphisms, which are in some sense “as far as possible to discrete”:

Definition 1.2.11
.

A geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is said to be
connected
if
f^{*}
is full and faithful.

Proposition 1.2.12

(
[
6
]
[Proposition C3.3.4])
.

Connected geometric morphisms are left biorthogonal to etale geometric morphisms.

Beware that not any geometric morphism that is left bi-orthogonal to etale geometric morphisms has to be connected; symmetrically, not any geometric morphism that is right bi-orthogonal to connected geometric morphism has to be etale: for instance, 2-cofiltered bilimits of etale geometric morphisms are so, though they are not etale. The complete bi-orthogonality structure involved here shall be described in the next section. However, there is a class of geometric morphisms for which connected and etale morphisms form altogether an bi-orthogonality structure:

Definition 1.2.13
.

A geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
is said to be
locally connected
if its inverse image part
f^{*}
admits an
indexed left adjoint

f_{!}
– see
[
6
]
[C3.3]. A Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
is
locally connected
if its terminal map is locally connected.

Remark 1.2.14
.

A geometric morphism is locally connected if and only if the inverse image part
f^{*}
admits a left adjoint
f_{!}
satisfying the
Frobenius identity

f_{!}(F\times_{f^{*}E}f^{*}E^{\prime})\simeq f_{!}F\times_{E}E^{\prime}
for any
a:F\rightarrow f^{*}E
in
\mathcal{F}
,
b:E^{\prime}\rightarrow E
in
\mathcal{E}
.

Remark 1.2.15
.

Any etale geometric morphism
\pi_{E}:\mathcal{E}/E\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is locally connected, with the further left adjoint of the projection
\pi_{E}:\mathcal{E}/E\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
given by postcomposition with the terminal map
!_{E}:E\rightarrow 1_{\mathcal{E}}
. However, not all connected geometric morphisms are locally connected.

In some sense, locally connected geometric morphisms are those that have a well behaved notion of object of connected components, represented by the essential image. In particular, the object
f_{!}(1_{\mathcal{F}})
can be seen as an object of connected components of
\mathcal{F}
seen as a topos over
\mathcal{E}
. Then the correspondence between global elements and etale factorization yields an initial factorization corresponding to the unit
1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}f_{!}(1_{\mathcal{F}})
of the terminal object:

Proposition 1.2.16

(
[
6
]
[Proposition C3.3.5(i)])
.

Any locally connected geometric morphism factorizes uniquely as a connected, locally connected geometric morphism followed by an etale geometric morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/f_{!}(1_{\mathcal{F}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{f!(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where
(c_{f})_{!}
sends
F
to the image of the terminal map
f_{!}(!_{F}):f_{!}(F)\rightarrow f_{!}(1_{\mathcal{F}})
.

1.3
(Terminally connected, etale) factorization of essential geometric morphisms

This factorization still makes sense if
f_{!}
only defines an ordinary, not necessarily indexed left adjoint to
f^{*}
, that is, when
f
is simply an
essential
geometric morphism, although the left part is no longer connected: it shall lie in a more general class of morphisms that are, intuitively, connected only from the “point of view” of the terminal object:

Definition 1.3.1
.

A geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is said to be
terminally connected
if its inverse image
f^{*}
lifts uniquely global elements, that is, if one has a natural isomorphism

\mathcal{F}[1_{\mathcal{F}},f^{*}(-)]\simeq\mathcal{E}[1_{\mathcal{E}},-]

Remark 1.3.2
.

This condition states that not only any global element
a:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow f^{*}(E)
comes from a unique global element
\overline{a}:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow E
in
\mathcal{E}
, but also that these lifts can be done functorially, in the sense that, for any
s:F_{1}\rightarrow F_{2}
and any equality of global elements
a_{2}=f^{*}(s)a_{1}
as encoded in a triangle as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{E}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}(F_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}(F_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}

then the lifts are related by the following commutative triangle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{a_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{a_{2}}}

In other words, we have an invertible 2-cell in the 2-category of categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Set}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Remark 1.3.3
.

In the setting above, the unique lift is moreover related through the unit as in the commutative triangles:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{E}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{*}f^{*}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{E}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}f_{*}f^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}f_{*}f^{*}\overline{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{E}}

The following observation is immediate by adjunction:

Proposition 1.3.4
.

An essential geometric morphism is terminally connected if and only if one has
f_{!}(1_{\mathcal{E}})\simeq 1_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proposition 1.3.5
.

A locally connected geometric morphism is terminally connected if and only if it is connected.

Proof.

The Frobenius identity of
remark

1.2.14
gives that for any
E
in
\mathcal{E}
one can retrieve the counit of the
f_{!}\dashv f^{*}
adjunctions as the composite
f_{!}f^{*}E\simeq f_{!}(1_{\mathcal{F}}\times f_{*}E)\simeq f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}\times E
. Hence, as soon as
f
is terminally connected, this gives
f_{!}f^{*}E\simeq E
, which exhibits
f^{*}
as fully faithful.
∎

A connected geometric morphism is terminally connected; beware that terminal connectedness is a weaker condition that connectedness, as it requires only full faithfulness relatively to global elements and not arbitrary generalized elements. Hence, not all terminally connected morphisms are connected: in a sense, the terminal object “believes” they are connected, although they might not be so from the point of view of other objects.

Proposition 1.3.6
.

Terminally connected geometric morphisms are left biorthogonal to etale geometric morphisms.

Proof.

Let be an invertible square as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

corresponding to a global element
a:1_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow t^{*}f^{*}E
in
\mathcal{G}
. If
t
is terminally connected, then this element comes uniquely from a global element
\overline{a}:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}E
in
\mathcal{F}
, whose name is a diagonalization of the pseudosquare above
\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}/E
. As any other possible diagonalization would name another antecedent of
a
, this diagonalization has to be unique. Now, let us examine the functoriality of the lift relative to morphisms of pseudosquares; a situation as below

\hbox to125.06pt{\vbox to69.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 62.5277pt\lower-30.23862pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
