{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.21667pt}{-27.87135pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -1.68 -38.57)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

defines a triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t^{*}f^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t^{*}f^{\prime*}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}\psi_{E}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}

which, by functoriality of the lifting property of
t
, must come uniquely from a morphism between global elements

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime*}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{E}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{a^{\prime}}}

which, in turn, exactly defines a morphism between the diagonalizations provided respectively by
\overline{a}
and
\overline{a^{\prime}}
.
∎

Proposition 1.3.7

(
[
2
]
[Proposition 4.62])
.

Any essential geometric morphism factorizes uniquely as a terminally connected geometric morphism followed by an etale geometric morphism.

Remark 1.3.8
.

In this case, terminal connectedness amounts for the essential image
f_{!}
to preserve the terminal object. Then the factorization is obtained as in the locally connected case as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}}}

where the left part is the name of the unit
\eta_{1_{\mathcal{F}}}:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}
.

The aim of this work is to generalize this latter result to arbitrary geometric morphisms. In absence of the essential image, the factorization shall not concentrate on a specific slice of the codomain topos; as pointed out in the above discussion about global elements, we shall have to consider rather a diagram consisting of possible factorizations through etale geometric morphisms.

2

Pro-etale geometric morphisms

Generalizing the (terminally connected, etale) factorization to arbitrary geometric morphisms will involve taking cofiltered bilimit of etale geometric morphisms indexed by categories of global elements. This implies some prerequisites on cofiltered bilimits of Grothendieck topoi.

2.1
Cofiltered bilimits of topoi and pro-etale geometric morphisms

Let us begin with a few recalls on cofilteredness and the associated notion of initialness.

Definition 2.1.1
.

Recall that a category
I
is said to be
cofiltered
if

-

it is not empty

-

for any to
i,j
in
I
there is a span
u:k\rightarrow i
,
v:k\rightarrow j

-

for any parallel pair
u,v:i\rightrightarrows j
there is
w:k\rightarrow i
such that
uw=vw
.

Remark 2.1.2
.

In particular, combining those two axioms, in a cofiltered category any cospan can be closed by a span into a commutative square.

Example 2.1.3
.

It is a standard fact that for any geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
and any object
F
of
\mathcal{F}
, the category
F\downarrow f^{*}
of
F
-indexed elements of
f^{*}
is cofiltered. In particular the category of global element
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}
is cofiltered.

Cofiltered categories have a special relation with
initial
functors, which are those that left limits unchanged when precomposing diagrams along them:

Definition 2.1.4
.

A functor
F:J\rightarrow I
is
initial
if for any
i
in
I
, the comma category
F\downarrow i
is non empty and connected. If
J
is a full subcategory
I
and its inclusion is initial, we say that
J
is initial in
I
.

Connectedness amounts to the possibility to exhibit a zig-zag in
J
connecting any two arrows from
F
to an object
i
in
I
. In the case where
I
is cofiltered, this condition simplifies:

Lemma 2.1.5
.

Let
I
be cofiltered. Then a functor
F:J\rightarrow I
is initial if and only if for any
i
in
J
, there is an arrow
F(j)\rightarrow i
and for any
d:F(j)\rightarrow i
,
d^{\prime}:F(j^{\prime})\rightarrow i
there is
t:j^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow j
,
t^{\prime}:j^{\prime}\rightarrow j
such that
dF(t)=d^{\prime}F(t^{\prime})
.

Lemma 2.1.6
.

A full subcategory
J
of a cofiltered category
I
is both initial and cofiltered as soon as any
i
in
I
admits a map
j\rightarrow i
.

Proof.

Suppose that any
i
admits a map
j\rightarrow i
with
j
in
J
. We show that
J\downarrow i
is connected. Take a cospan
d:j\rightarrow i
,
d^{\prime}:j^{\prime}\rightarrow i
: as
I
is cofiltered there exists a span
b:i^{\prime}\rightarrow j
,
b^{\prime}:i^{\prime}\rightarrow j^{\prime}
in
I
such that
db=d^{\prime}b^{\prime}
; now there is by hypothesis an arrow
t:j^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow i^{\prime}
, and by fullness of
J
, the composites
bt
,
b^{\prime}t
exhibit
J\downarrow i
as connected. Cofilteredness of
J
is immediate.
∎

It is well known that the bicategory of Grothendieck topoi admits small cofiltered bilimits. In
[
4
]
is provided the following site-theoretic description: suppose one has a cofilftered diagram of Grothendieck topoi
\mathbb{E}:I\rightarrow{\bf GTop}
(denoting
\mathbb{E}(i)
as
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
\mathbb{E}(u)
as
f_{u}:\mathcal{E}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{j}
for
u:i\rightarrow j
) with
I
a small cofiltered category. Suppose moreover we have for each
i
a lex site
(\mathcal{C}_{i},J_{i})
for
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and for each
u
a morphism of sites
\overline{f}_{u}:\mathcal{C}_{j}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{i}
such that
f_{u}
is induced by
\overline{f}_{u}
: in other words, require that the diagram
\mathcal{E}
is induced from a filtered diagram of lex sites
\mathbb{C}:I^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\textbf{LexSites}
such that
\mathbb{E}\simeq{\bf Sh}\circ\mathbb{C}
. It is known that the category of
LexSites
admits filtered pseudocolimits: the pseudocolimit
{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}_{i}
in
{\bf Lex}
can be endowed with a coarsest topology
\bigvee_{i\in I}q_{i}(J_{i})
generated by the image of the covering families in all the
J_{i}
along the pseudocolimits inclusions
q_{i}
; this latter makes those inclusions morphisms of lex sites, and exhibits the pseudocolimit as a pseudocolimit in
LexSites
. Then from
[
4
]
we know the following:

Proposition 2.1.7

(
[
4
]
Theorem 2.4)
.

In the situation above, the bilimit in
{\bf GTop}
of the cofiltered diagram
\mathbb{E}
is the sheaf topos

\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}_{i}\simeq{\bf Sh}(\underset{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{pscolim}}}\,\mathcal{C}_{i},\bigvee_{i\in I}q_{i}(J_{i}))

with the bilimit projections having as inverse image part the pseudocolimit inclusions
q_{i}
.

Remark 2.1.8
.

As pointed out in
[
4
]
[Theorem 2.5], one can actually dispense oneself from choosing a
small
site for the topoi and take directly the pseudocolimit of the topoi themselves equiped with their canonical topology together with the inverse image part of the diagram. In the case where the specified topology are subcanonical, we have the following:

Lemma 2.1.9
.

In the situation above, if the toplogies
J_{i}
are all subcanonical, then the induced topology
\bigvee_{i\in I}q_{i}(J_{i})
is subcanonical also.

Proof.

A topology is subcanonical if it is contained in the canonical topology; hence so is the join of subcanonical topologies, as their join is the smallest one containing them.
∎

We can now introduce the class of geometric morphisms that will play the right class in our factorization theorem:

Definition 2.1.10
.

A geometric morphism will be called
pro-etale
if if it is equivalent in
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
to a cofiltered bilimit of etale morphisms over
\mathcal{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,\mathcal{E}/E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,\pi_{E_{i}}}

for a cofiltered diagram
\mathbb{E}:I\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
.

Remark 2.1.11
.

A pro-etale can hence be presented (not necessarily in a unique way) by an invertible 2-cell in
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,\mathcal{E}/E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,\pi_{E_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}

where
e
is a geometric equivalence, and
\alpha
an invertible 2-cell.

Moreover, such a
p
comes then equiped with a canonical pseudocone provided by the pasting of the presentation 2-cell
(e,\alpha)
with the bilimit projections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,\mathcal{E}/E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,\pi_{E_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{i}\atop\simeq}

with
p_{d}:\mathcal{E}/up_{i}\simeq p_{j}
the invertible transitions 2-cell at
d:i\rightarrow j
.

Remark 2.1.12
.

For any geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
one has an equivalence of categories

{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}[f,\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\pi_{E_{i}}]\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}[f,\pi_{E_{i}}]

This means that a morphism
(x,\alpha):f\Rightarrow{\textup{bilim}}_{i\in I}\pi_{E_{i}}
in
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
defines in an essentially unique way a family
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
of global elements
a_{i}:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}E_{i}
encoded by the composites
(p_{i},\gamma_{i})(e,\alpha)
in
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
, satisfying the coherence condition that for any
d:i\rightarrow j
one has a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}u_{d}}

encoded by the inverse image part of the invertible 2-cell obtained as the whiskering

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{i}^{*}u^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{j}^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{d}*f)^{\flat}\atop\simeq}

Moreover, the cofilteredness condition ensures that for any
i,i^{\prime}
there is a span
d:j\rightarrow i
,
d^{\prime}:j\rightarrow i^{\prime}
such that
a_{i}=f^{*}u_{d}a_{j}
and
a_{i^{\prime}}=f^{*}u_{d^{\prime}}a_{j}
, and similar conditions about the equalization of parallel cells. In particular, in the cofiltered diagram
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}
such solutions are provided by the inverse images of the product and the equalizers computed in
\mathcal{E}
.

Corollary 2.1.13
.

Pro-etale geometric morphisms over
\mathcal{E}
are discrete objects in
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

We saw at
remark

1.2.9
that each homcategory
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}[f,\pi_{E}]
is in turn equivalent to the set
\mathcal{F}[1_{\mathcal{F}},f^{*}E]
. But as the bilimit is indexed by a 1-category
I
, the bilimit of those homset is still equivalent to a set.
∎

Remark 2.1.14
.

Observe that in the definition of pro-etale morphisms, we work in the pseudoslice
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
, where in particular the cofiltered bilimit is computed; but as cofiltered bilimits are connected, they are preserved by the slice projection
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf GTop}
, hence the domain of a pro-etale geometric morphisms is the bilimit of the associated slice topoi. Moreover, its universal property is not very far from the one in the slice:

{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}[\mathcal{F},\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/{E_{i}}]\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E}/{E_{i}}]

As the bilimit cocone is a pseudococone with invertible transition 2-cells
p_{d}:\mathcal{E}/u_{d}p_{i}\simeq p_{j}
at
d:i\rightarrow j
– which are however not required to be identities – the composite of a morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow{\textup{bilim}}_{i\in I}\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
with this pseudococone begets a family
(p_{i}f)_{i\in I}
and each of the composites
\pi_{E_{i}}p_{i}f
is equivalent to
{\textup{bilim}}_{i\in I}\pi_{E_{i}}f
in
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
, so it defines a coherent family of element of
{\textup{bilim}}_{i\in I}\pi_{E_{i}}f
. Conversely for a pseudocone
(f_{i}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
each
f_{i}
code a global element of the corresponding composite
p_{i}f_{i}
, although at first sight those composites do not define a unique morphism from
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{E}
, although they are equivalent to each other. In fact, they actually do: they are global elements of the composite
{\textup{bilim}}_{i\in I}\pi_{E_{i}}(f_{i})_{i\in I}
where
(f_{i})_{i\in I}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow{\textup{bilim}}_{i\in I}\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
is the universal morphism induced by the bilimit of the slice topoi. All of this ensures that actually the pro-etale morphism
{\textup{bilim}}_{i\in I}\pi_{E_{i}}
and its domain
{\textup{bilim}}_{i\in I}\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
contains exactly the same information.

Remark 2.1.15
.

Let us describe in all details what gives the expression of
proposition

2.1.7
in the case of the domain of a pro-etale geometric morphism. If
\mathcal{E}
has
(\mathcal{C},J)
as a small lex, subcanonical site of definition, then for each object
C
of
\mathcal{C}
, a site for
\mathcal{E}/\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}
can be constructed by
(\mathcal{C}/C,J_{C})
, where
J_{C}
is the relativised topology generated by pullbacks of
J
-covers along
C
– and this site is subcanonical since
J
was chosen so. Suppose now that the objects
E_{i}
are chosen as arising from representable
\text{\char 136\relax}_{C_{i}}
; for etale geometric morphisms enjoy left cancellation, all the transition morphisms in the diagram must be etale, but then they correspond to morphisms in
\mathcal{E}
between the corresponding objects, which, by full faithfulness of the Yoneda embedding, come uniquely from morphisms
C_{i}\rightarrow C_{j}
in
\mathcal{C}
: we thus obtain a cofiltered diagram
(C_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}
with
u_{d}
for
d:i\rightarrow j
in
I
as transition morphisms.

This defines an indexed site
I^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
sending
i
on
(\mathcal{C}/C_{i},J_{C_{i}})
with the pullback functors
u_{d}^{*}:\mathcal{C}/C_{j}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
as transition morphisms for
d:i\rightarrow j
- which are morphisms of lex sites by cancellation of pullbacks. Moreover,
I^{{\rm op}}
is filtered. Now recall that we can compute the filtered pseudocolimit
{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
in
{\bf Cat}
as a localization of the oplax colimit
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
at the cartesian morphisms associated to the fibration
\pi:{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}\rightarrow I
. This can be described as a category of fractions: its objects are pairs
(i,h)
with
i
in
I
and
h:D\rightarrow C_{i}
in
\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
, while a morphism
(i_{0},h_{0})\rightarrow(i_{1},h_{1})
consists of a span of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i^{\prime},u_{d}^{*}h_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{1},h_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{0},h_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1},g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{0},1_{u_{d_{0}}^{*}h_{0}})}

with
d_{0}:i^{\prime}\rightarrow i_{0}
,
d_{1}:i^{\prime}\rightarrow i_{1}
in
I
and
g
satisfying the commutation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{d_{0}}^{*}D_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{i_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{d_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{d_{0}}^{*}h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

The pseudocolimit
{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
is small and lex because
I
is cofiltered, and
{\bf Lex}
has filtered pseudocolimits which are moreover computed in
{\bf Cat}
, see
[
3
]
[Lemma 5.3.2] for instance. Moreover, the oplax colimit is itself equiped with a
vertical topology
generated from the fiberwise topologies
\bigvee_{i\in I}\iota_{i}(J_{C_{i}})
. One can show that the localization
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}\rightarrow{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
is a lex-morphism of site which induces a topology on the pseudocolimit, which we will denote the same way. Moreover, the pseudocolimit inclusions
q_{i}:\mathcal{C}/C_{i}\rightarrow{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
define morphisms of lex sites.

Then the cofiltered bilimit
{\textup{bilim}}_{i\in I}\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
has the following presentation

\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,\mathcal{E}/E_{i}\simeq{\bf Sh}(\underset{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{pscolim}}}\,\mathcal{C}/C_{i},\bigvee_{i\in I}\iota_{i}(J_{C_{i}}))

where the bilimit projections are the geometric morphisms induced from the morphisms of lex site
q_{i}
at the underlying pseudocolimit inclusion. In particular, as the induced topologies on the slice
J_{C_{i}}
are subcanonical, so is the generated topology.

Remark 2.1.16
.

In the previous remark, we suppose that the objects
E_{i}
and the morphisms between them in the diagram in
\mathcal{E}
arise from objects and morphisms inside a small standard site for
\mathcal{E}
. For
I
is small, it is always possible to find such a site of presentation once the diagram in
\mathcal{E}
is given. Hence, any pro-etale geometric morphism can be presented in such a way.

However, this precaution is not necessary: we can also simply equip
\mathcal{E}
with its canonical topology, and the induced topology
J_{E_{i}}
induced on each slice by the pullback functor
E_{i}^{*}:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
coincides with the canonical topology on
\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
for colimits are stable in Grothendieck topoi. Now from
remark

2.1.8
, we can take as a site for the bilimit the
large
site

{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
equiped with the smallest topology making the pseudocolimit inclusions continuous.

Remark 2.1.17
.

One can check that the class of cartesian morphisms in
{\textup{pscolim}}_{I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
possesses a
right calculus of fractions
; let us examine carefully how it manifests. First, we know that the class of cartesian morphisms is closed under composition. For the right Ore condition: consider a situation as below (so that the right leg is cartesian and
a
is a morphism
a:h_{1}\rightarrow d_{1}^{*}h_{0}
in
\mathcal{C}/C_{i_{1}}
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{2},d_{2}^{*}h_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{1},h_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{0},h_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{2},1_{d_{2}^{*}h_{0}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1},a)}

then for
I
is cofiltered, one can close this span into a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{2}}

and then close the span above into a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,s_{1}^{*}h_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{2},d_{2}^{*}h_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{1},h_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{0},h_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{2},1_{d_{2}^{*}h_{0}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1},a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s_{1},1_{s_{1}^{*}h_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s_{2},s_{1}^{*}a)}

where the upper leg is obtained from
s_{1}^{*}a:s_{1}^{*}h_{1}\rightarrow s_{1}^{*}d_{1}^{*}h_{0}=s_{2}^{*}d_{2}^{*}h_{0}
and the left leg is cartesian.

The equalization condition is trivially fullfiled: indeed, in the case of a coequalizing fork

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,h_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,d^{*}h_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j^{\prime},h_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1},a_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{2},a_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d,1_{d^{*}h_{1}})}

then
dd_{1}=dd_{2}
and
a_{1}=a_{2}:h_{0}\rightarrow d_{1}^{*}d^{*}h_{1}=d_{2}^{*}d^{*}h_{1}
so the identity of
(i,h_{0})
, which is cartesian, equalizes trivially
(d_{1},a_{1})
and
(d_{2},a_{2})
. As a consequence, we can really see the pseudocolimit
{\textup{pscolim}}_{I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
as a category of right fractions. This will allow for easier manipulation of the morphisms, and in particular global elements, in the pseudocolimit and in the bilimit topos it generates.

2.2
Canonical indexing of a pro-etale geometric morphism

Alhough pro-etale morphisms may be presented by a given cofiltered diagram of etale morphisms, there is always a canonical way to reindex them thanks to their category of global elements. In this subsection we explain the procedure.

For a pro-etale geometric morphism
p:{\textup{bilim}}_{i\in I}\mathcal{E}/C_{i}
associated to a diagram
\mathbb{C}
in a standard site
(\mathcal{C},J)
for
\mathcal{E}
, the inverse image
p^{*}
restricts to a morphism of site
\overline{p}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
sending
C
to the equivalence class of the projection at any index
[(i,C_{i}^{*}C)]_{\sim}
and a morphism
u:C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
to the morphism represented by any of the vertical morphisms of the form
(1_{i},1_{C_{i}}\times u):(i,C_{i}^{*}C_{1})\rightarrow(i,C_{i}^{*}C_{2})
. Now let us explain how we can reindex the limit decomposition of a pro-etale geometric morphism on its own category of elements.

This requires some sanity-check on the nature of global elements in
{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
. There is a terminal object
1{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
given by the class of any terminal element in the fibers
[(i,1_{C_{i}})]_{\sim}
for any choice of
i
. Then the global elements are also easy to represent:

Lemma 2.2.1
.

Any global element
1_{{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}}\rightarrow\overline{p}(C)
can be represented by a vertical morphism
(i,1_{C_{i}})\rightarrow(i,C_{i}^{*}C)
in
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{i\in I^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}
.

Proof.

Recall that morphisms in the pseudocolimit are represented as fractions with a cartesian morphism on the left: that is, if we start with a representant
(i,C_{i}^{*}C)
for
\overline{p}(C)
and a representant
(j,1_{C_{j}})
for the terminal object, then a morphism between them is a span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k,h)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,C_{i}^{*}C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,1_{C_{j}})}}

with the left leg cartesian: but then, we must have
h=1_{C_{k}}
for transitions morphisms are lex; on the other hand, for we have a (vertical, cartesian) factorization system in the oplax colimit, the right leg can be chosen to be vertical, that is, we can chose
k=i
. Then we have a representing morphism
(i,1_{C_{i}})\rightarrow(i,C_{i}^{*}C)
, that is, a section of the product projection
C_{i}\times C\rightarrow C_{i}
, which is also the same as a morphism
a:C_{i}\rightarrow C
.
∎

Now, let us reindex the presenting bilimit of our pro-etale geometric morphism thanks to global elements. Define the functor
\widetilde{p}:I\rightarrow 1_{{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}}\downarrow\overline{p}
as sending
i
to the class of the element corresponding to the diagonal

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(i,1_{C_{i}})]_{\sim}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(i,C_{i}^{*}C_{i})]_{\sim}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[(1_{i},\Delta_{C_{i}})]_{\sim}}

Lemma 2.2.2
.

The functor
\widetilde{p}:I\rightarrow 1_{{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}}\downarrow\overline{p}
is initial.

Proof.

Take a global element of some
\overline{p}(C)
in the pseudocolimit, induced from a
a:C_{i}\rightarrow C
as chosen in
lemma

2.2.1
as the corresponding
[(1_{i},(1_{C_{i}},a))]_{\sim}
. We must prove that the category
\widetilde{p}\downarrow(C,[(1_{i},(1_{C_{i}},a))]_{\sim})
is non-empty and connected. Non-emptiness is witnessed by the existence of the triangle over
C_{i}
provided by property of the diagonal from the data given by
a
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{i}\times C_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{i}\times C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{i}^{*}C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{C_{i}}\times a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{i}^{*}C_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{C_{i}},a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{C_{i}}}

For
1_{{\textup{pscolim}}_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}/C_{i}}\downarrow\overline{p}
is cofiltered by flatness of
\overline{p}
, if suffices to prove that any two parallel morphisms of elements
\widetilde{p}(j)\rightrightarrows(C,[(1_{i},a)]_{\sim})
are equalized by some morphism
\widetilde{p}(d):\widetilde{p}(j^{\prime})\rightarrow\widetilde{p}(j)
. A parallel pair will be presented by a square of the following form, with the upper isomorphism relating two presentations of the terminal object suited to give vertical presentation of the corresponding global elements:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(j,1_{C_{j}})]_{\sim}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(i,1_{C_{i}})]_{\sim}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(j,C_{j}^{*}C_{j})]_{\sim}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(i,C_{i}^{*}C)]_{\sim}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[(1_{j},\Delta_{C_{j}})]_{\sim}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[(d_{1},1_{C_{j}}\times b_{1})]_{\sim}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[(1_{i},a)]_{\sim}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[(d_{2},1_{C_{j}}\times b_{2})]_{\sim}}

The lower parallel pair corresponds through the right calculus of fractions to a pair of spans as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k_{1},C_{k_{1}}^{*}C_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,C_{j}^{*}C_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,C_{i}^{*}C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k_{2},C_{k_{2}}^{*}C_{j})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1},1_{C_{k_{1}}}\times b_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{2},1_{C_{k_{2}}}\times b_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s_{1},1_{C_{k_{1}}^{*}C_{j}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s_{2},1_{C_{k_{2}}^{*}C_{j}})}

with
b_{1},b_{2}:C_{j}\rightarrow C
a parallel pair. For
I
is cofiltered, we know there exists a span
t_{1}:j^{\prime}\rightarrow k_{1}
,
t_{2}:j^{\prime}\rightarrow k_{2}
such that
s_{1}t_{1}=s_{2}t_{2}
and
d_{1}t_{1}=d_{2}t_{2}
. Then in
\mathcal{C}/C_{j^{\prime}}
both
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
induce the same map
(1_{C_{j^{\prime}}},f_{s_{1}}f_{t_{1}})=(1_{C_{j^{\prime}}},f_{s_{2}}f_{t_{2}}):1_{C_{j^{\prime}}}\rightarrow C_{j^{\prime}}^{*}C_{j}
while the data of
(d_{1},1_{C_{k_{1}}}\times b_{1}),(d_{2},1_{C_{k_{2}}}\times b_{2})
are transferred to a pair of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}/C_{j^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{j^{\prime}}^{*}C_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{j^{\prime}}^{*}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{t_{1}}^{*}(1_{C_{k_{1}}}\times b_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{t_{2}}^{*}(1_{C_{k_{2}}}\times b_{2})}

But those later are actually nothing but
1_{C_{j^{\prime}}}\times b_{1}
and
1_{C_{j^{\prime}}}\times b_{2}
respectively, and the condition above states that one has an equality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{C_{j^{\prime}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{j^{\prime}}^{*}C_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{j^{\prime}}^{*}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{C_{j^{\prime}}}\times b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{C_{j^{\prime}}}\times b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{C_{j^{\prime}}},f_{s_{1}}f_{t_{1}})=(1_{C_{j^{\prime}}},f_{s_{2}}f_{t_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{t_{1}}^{*}f_{d_{1}}^{*}a=f_{t_{2}}^{*}f_{d_{2}}^{*}a}

and as all those maps live in
\mathcal{C}/C_{j}
,
a
has to be uniquely induced from some
b:C_{j}\rightarrow C
and one has
b_{1}=b_{2}=b
. This forces the corresponding parallel morphisms we started with to be actually equal. ∎

As a consequence, though pro-etale may be presented as indexed by arbitrary small cofiltered diagrams, they always possess a canonical presentation indexed by the category of global elements of their inverse image:

Corollary 2.2.3
.

Any pro-etale geometric morphism
p:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is equivalent in
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
to the pro-etale geometric morphism indexed by the category of global elements of its inverse image: we have a geometric equivalence over
\mathcal{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow p^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ulcorner a\urcorner)_{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow p^{*}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow p^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\pi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

3

(Terminally connected, pro-etale) factorization of geometric morphisms

In this section, we prove that pro-etale geometric morphisms are the correct generalization of etale geometric morphisms when extending the (terminally connected, etale) factorization to arbitrary geometric morphisms. This factorization shall also be a generalization of the construction of the
germ
of a topos at a point, as being obtained as a cofiltered limit of all etale factorizations of this morphism.

3.1
Some preliminary observations

The following fact is an immediate consequence of closure properties of right classes under limits in the pseudoslices, since terminally connected geometric morphisms are left bi-orthogonal to etale ones:

Lemma 3.1.1
.

Pro-etale geometric morphisms are right bi-orthogonal to terminally connected geometric morphisms.

Proof.

We saw that terminally connected geometric morphisms are left bi-orthogonal to etale geometric morphisms. Since a right class in a 2-orthogonality structure is closed under bilimits, pro-etale geometric morphisms are ensured to be also right bi-orthogonal to terminally connected geometric morphisms.
∎

We are going to consider a bilimit indexed by the category of all global elements
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}
of a geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
; as this category is large, we must exhibit a small initial category inside of it to ensure the existence of the bilimit. We process by the following accessibility argument.

It is known, see for instance
[
1
]
[Proposition 3.4.16], that Grothendieck topoi are locally presentable and can be exhibited as a lex localization for the category of
{\bf Set}
-valued sheaves over a generator of
\mu
-compact objects for some sufficiently large
\mu
. Moreover, in a locally presentable category, any object becomes compact above a certain cardinal.

Lemma 3.1.2
.

Let be
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
a geometric morphism. Then there exists a small site
(\mathcal{C},J)
such that the category
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}
of global elements of the form
1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}(c)
for
c
in
\mathcal{C}
is cofiltered and initial in the category
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}
.

Proof.

For
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{F}
is in particular locally presentable, and hence, there exists some cardinal
\kappa
such that
1_{\mathcal{F}}
is
\kappa
-compact. On the other side, as
\mathcal{E}
is also locally presentable, there exists some cardinal
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{E}
is locally
\lambda
-presentable; hence, it suffices to take
\mu\geq\kappa,\lambda
, so that we simultaneously have
1_{\mathcal{F}}
is
\mu
-compact and the subcategory
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
of
\mu
-compact objects generates
\mathcal{E}
under
\mu
-filtered colimits. Then, there exists a Grothendieck topology
J_{\mu}
on
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
such that
(\mathcal{E}_{\mu},J_{\mu})
is a small site of definition for
\mathcal{E}
. In fact,
J_{\mu}
is the restriction of the canonical topology of
\mathcal{E}
to
\mu
-compact objects.

But now, for any
F
in
\mathcal{E}
, we have a
\mu
-filtered colimit
F\simeq{\textup{colim}}\,\iota_{\mu}\downarrow F
, where
\iota_{\mu}:\mathcal{E}_{\mu}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}
is the dense inclusion of
\mu
-compact objects. As
1_{\mathcal{F}}
is
\mu
-compact while
f^{*}
preserves colimits, any global element
a:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}F
lifts as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}(K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(x)}

for some
x:K\rightarrow F
with
K
a
\mu
-compact object in
\mathcal{E}
. This exactly means that any global element
(F,a)
admits a morphism
x:(K,b)\rightarrow(F,a)
with
K

\mu
-compact. But now, since
f^{*}
was the inverse image part of a geometric morphism, the category of global element
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}
is cofiltered. Then, by
lemma

2.1.6
,
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}\iota_{\mu}
is itself cofiltered and initial in
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}
. ∎

Corollary 3.1.3
.

For any geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, the large cofiltered bilimit
{\textup{bilim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E
exists in the 2-category of Grothendieck topoi.

Proof.

From
lemma

3.1.2
, we know that there always exists a small cofinal category of elements of the restriction of the inverse image of
f
to a convenient small site
(\mathcal{C},J)
of presentation. Then we are ensured to have an equivalence of categories

\underset{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E\simeq\underset{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}

where the later cofiltered bilimit is ensured to exist as it is small indexed.
∎

3.2
The factorization theorem

We are now reaching the central result of this paper, establishing the promised factorization theorem:

Theorem 3.2.1
.

Any geometric morphism factorizes in an essentially unique way as a terminally connected geometric morphism followed by a pro-etale geometric morphism.

Proof.

This is the main proof of this paper and deserves a quick overview of our strategy. After recalling the correspondence of etale factorizations with global elements of the inverse image, we propose a pro-etale candidate on the right, constructed as a cofiltered bilimit indexed by such global elements. Then we present it through a canonical pseudocolimit of sites, and describe global elements in this bilimit topos. The bilimit property induces a universal map on the left: we first discuss its action on objects of the pseudocolimit site, and then how it extends to arbitrary objects in the associated bilimit topos; we finally show it lifts uniquely global elements, ensuring the associated geometric morphism to be terminally connected. In a sense, the key argument is that this factorization “displays” all possible global elements of the inverse image of the given geometric morphism, so that the residual left part must reflect them faithfully.

Let be
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
be a geometric morphism. We saw at
1.2.8
that we have an equivalence of categories

1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}\simeq f\downarrow\textbf{Et}/\mathcal{F}

We are going to consider the following large bilimit
{\textup{bilim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E
indexed over all possible global elements
a:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}E
without restriction to any site of presentation for
\mathcal{E}
. From
corollary

3.1.3
, we know this bilimit to be correctly defined. As
f^{*}
is flat,
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}
is cofiltered: hence the bilimit morphism
\pi_{f}:{\textup{bilim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is pro-etale. Moreover, we know from
remark

2.1.8
that it is presented as

\underset{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E\simeq{\bf Sh}(\underset{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}{{\textup{pscolim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E,\bigvee_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}q_{(E,a)}(J_{E}))

where the
q_{(E,a)}:\mathcal{E}/E\rightarrow{\textup{pscolim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E
are the pseudocolimit inclusions.

On the other side, the correspondance above between global elements and factorizations of
f
yields a cone
(a:f\rightarrow\pi_{E})_{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}
in
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
, which induces uniquely a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

together at each element
(E,a)
an invertible 2-cell, provided by the inversal property of the bilimit, factorizing the name of the element
a
through the composite of
l_{f}
and the pro-etale part:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{(E,a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Now we must prove
l_{f}
to be terminally connected. Let us first give a word about its action at the level of the pseudocolimit site of presentation of the bilimit. Recall that the pseudocolimit
{\textup{pscolim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E
, as a filtered pseudocolimit of lex-categories, is computed in
{\bf Cat}
as the localization of the oplax colimit at cartesian morphisms: in other words, its objects are equivalence classes of triples
(E,a,h)
with
(E,a)
a global element of
f^{*}
and
h:D\rightarrow E
an object of
\mathcal{E}/E
. Moreover, the restriction of
l_{f}^{*}
to this site can be computed as applying the corresponding
a^{*}
to an object of the site presented by a triple
(E,a,h)
which returns a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{l_{f}^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}_{[(E,a,h)]_{\sim}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

which is, as explained at
1.2.8
, the pullback of
a
and
f^{*}h
over
f^{*}E
. Similarly, for a morphism in the oplax colimit
(u,h):(E_{1},a_{1},h_{1})\rightarrow(E_{2},a_{2},h_{2})
corresponding to a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

the value of
l_{f}^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}_{[(u,h)]_{\sim}}
is given by morphism induced from the universal property of the pullback. This expression is well defined in the sense that it does not depend on the choice of the representant. Indeed, two triples
(E_{1},a_{1},h_{1})
and
(E_{2},a_{2},h_{2})
are identified in the pseudocolimit if and only if they are related by a cartesian morphism
(u,{\rm id}_{u^{*}h_{2}}):(E_{1},a_{1},h_{1})\rightarrow(E_{2},a_{2},h_{2})
, that is, if one has a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where
u
moreover provides a morphism of elements
(E_{1},a_{1})\rightarrow(E_{2},a_{2})
. This ensures, by cancellation of pullbacks, that in the following diagram the left face of the prism is also a pullback, ensuring in turn that
l_{f}^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}_{[(E_{1},a_{1},h_{1})]_{\sim}}\simeq l_{f}^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}_{[(E_{2},a_{2},h_{2})]_{\sim}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}D_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{2}^{*}f^{*}D_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}D_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}_{2}f^{*}h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Before deducing from those observations that
l_{f}^{*}
is terminally connected, that is, lifts uniquely global elements, we should first give a word on what global elements in the pseudocolimit are – we shall see that those coming from the pseudocolimit shall be enough to lift global element of arbitrary object in the bilimit topos. The terminal object of
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}\mathcal{E}/E}\mathcal{E}/E
is the pair
(1_{\mathcal{E}},{\rm id}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}})
and the terminal map of any
(E,a,h)
has as underlying morphism of elements the following triangle together with the terminal square as second component

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}(1_{\mathcal{E}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm id}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(!_{E})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{E}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{E}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{D}}

Now, recall that cartesian morphisms in the oplax colimit enjoy a right calculus of fraction, so that a morphism
1_{{\textup{pscolim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E}\rightarrow[(E,a,h)]_{\sim}
in the localization
{\textup{pscolim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E
can be presented as a span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(D,b,{\rm id}_{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(E,a,h)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1_{\mathcal{E}},{\rm id}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}},{\rm id}_{1_{\mathcal{E}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u,h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(!_{D},!_{D})}

where the vertical leg is cartesian, which expresses the fact that any object of the form
(D,b,{\rm id}_{D})
provides a representing object for the terminal object as pullbacks send identity maps on identity maps. In other words, any arrow
(u,h)
as above shall provide a global element of
[(E,a,h)]_{\sim}
in the pseudocolimit, whatever
(D,b)
was chosen as.

We can prove now
l_{f}
to be terminally connected. The inverse image part of
l_{f}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow{\textup{bilim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E
is induced from the pullback functor
l_{f}^{*}:{\textup{pscolim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
through the left Kan extension. Also, as
l_{f}^{*}
preserves colimit and any object in
{\bf Sh}({\textup{pscolim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E,\bigvee_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}q_{(E,a,h)}(J_{E}))
is a colimit of objects from the presentation site, the action of
l_{f}^{*}
on arbitrary objects can be computed as a colimit of inverse images of basic objects. Concretely, if
X\simeq{\textup{colim}}_{I_{X}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{[(E_{i},a_{i},h_{i})]_{\sim}}
in
{\textup{bilim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E
, then as
f^{*}
preserves colimits, we have

l_{f}^{*}X\simeq\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;l_{f}^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}_{[(E_{i},a_{i},h_{i})]_{\sim}}

But each
l_{f}^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}_{[(E_{i},a_{i},h_{i})]_{\sim}}
is computed as the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{l_{f}^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}_{[(E_{i},a_{i},h_{i})]_{\sim}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}D_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

As colimits are pullback stable in Grothendieck topoi, the following square is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;l_{f}^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}_{[(E_{i},a_{i},h_{i})]_{\sim}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;f^{*}D_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;f^{*}E_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;a_{i}^{*}f^{*}h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;f^{*}h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

As
f^{*}
preserves colimits, this pullback expresses nothing but the fact that
l_{f}^{*}
behaves on objects as it does on basic elements, that is, by taking fibers:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{l_{f}^{*}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;D_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;E_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

But then, by the universal property of the pullback, a global element
b:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow l_{f}^{*}X
of
l_{f}^{*}
induces a morphism between global elements of
f^{*}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;D_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;E_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;a_{i})\pi_{1}b}

which defines itself a morphism in the oplax colimit

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;D_{i},\pi_{2}b,{\rm id}_{\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;D_{i}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;E_{i},(\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;a_{i})\pi_{1}b,\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;h_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;h_{i},{\rm id}_{\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;D_{i}})}

The codomain of this morphism is conveniently sent to
1_{\mathcal{F}}
by
l_{f}^{*}
as seen in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;D_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;D_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

while
b
is recovered as the morphism induced between the image as depicted below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{l_{f}^{*}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;D_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;D_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;E_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underset{I_{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;a_{i})\pi_{1}b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

In other words,
l_{f}^{*}
lifts uniquely global elements of arbitrary objects, and
l_{f}
is hence terminally connected. As this was proven without specification of a site of presentation for
\mathcal{E}
, the factorization is ensured not to depend on a specific presentation.
∎

3.3
Laxness conditions

In general, for a given bifactorization system, the middle terms of the factorizations of the domain and codomain 1-cells of a non-invertible globular 2-cell may not be canonically related in any way: some factorization systems indeed lack such “lax functoriality”. However, it happens that the (terminally-connected, pro-etale) factorization system does enjoy such a structure.

Let be a globular 2-cell in
{\bf GTop}
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

It corresponds to a natural transformation
\phi^{\flat}:f^{*}\Rightarrow g^{*}
. At the level of global elements, this natural transformation induces a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow g^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}

sending a global element
a:1\rightarrow f^{*}E
to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}_{E}a}

This induces a 2-cell between the names of the corresponding elements

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{a}}

whose inverse image component at an object
h:D\rightarrow E
in
\mathcal{E}/E
is given by is the comparison map between the corresponding fibers

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\phi^{\flat}_{E}a)^{*}g^{*}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{*}f^{*}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}f^{*}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi^{\flat}_{E}a)^{*}g^{*}h}

This 2-cell actually provides a decomposition of
\phi
as the following pasting

\hbox to98.56pt{\vbox to40.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 49.27771pt\lower-20.29869pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
