Let us see how the pseudolimit formula for the comprehensive factorization interacts with those data. On the pro-etale side, the functor
\phi^{*}
defines a transformation of pseudocones

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow g^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

inducing a geometric morphism between the pseudolimits

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(E,b)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow g^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the morphism
\widetilde{\phi}
is induced from the morphism between the (large) pseudocolimits of sites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}{{\textup{pscolim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(E,b)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow g^{*}}{{\textup{pscolim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}}

sending an object represented by a triple
((E,a),h)
to the object represented by the triple
(E,\phi^{\flat}_{E}a),h)
.

On the terminally connected side, the data of the
(\phi^{a})_{a\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}
, as defined above from the pullback property, induce together with
\overline{\phi}
a canonical 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}{{\textup{pscolim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(E,b)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow g^{*}}{{\textup{pscolim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle b^{*}\rangle_{(E,b)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow g^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle a^{*}\rangle_{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi^{a})_{(E,b)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow g^{*}}}

whose associated geometric transformation can be notated
\lambda_{\phi}
. Combining those data provides us with a canonical decomposition of the globular 2-cell
\phi
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(E,b)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow g^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Hence, we can say that the (terminally connected, pro-etale) factorization system enjoys
oplax factorization of globular cells
, a notion which shall be developed in a future work. As a consequence, we can deduce a stronger orthogonality property of the terminally connected morphismes left to pro-etale:

Proposition 3.3.1
.

For any pair
t
,
p
with
t
terminally connected and
p
pro-etale, any oplax-square as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

admits a canonical decomposition of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\phi}\atop\simeq}

with an invertible 2-cell on the right.

Proof.

Take the oplax factorization of
\phi
seen as a globular cell
pg\Rightarrow ft
; as precomposing with a terminally connected geometric morphism (respectively, post-composing with a pro-etale geometric morphism) does not modify the factorization, we get a decomposition of
\phi
as the following pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{1_{\mathcal{G}}\downarrow g^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,\mathcal{H}/H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,\mathcal{E}/E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\phi}}

∎

Remark 3.3.2
.

It is not a coincidence that the other main example of 2-dimensional factorization system enjoying such a lax orthogonality property is the comprehensive factorization system on
{\bf Cat}
. We are discussing below some conjectures about this similarity. Such laxness property also often comes with additional stability condition along bicomma and bicocomma squares:

Proposition 3.3.3
.

Terminally connected geometric morphisms are stable left to bicocomma: that is, in a bicocomma square as below in
{\bf GTop}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\uparrow t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{f,t}}

if
t
is terminally connected, then so is
p_{1}
.

Proof.

Recall that the bicocomma square
f\uparrow t
in
{\bf GTop}
has as underlying category the bicomma square
f^{*}\uparrow t^{*}
in
{\bf Cat}
and is hence equiped with a universal 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}\downarrow t^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}_{f,t}}

where the category
f^{*}\downarrow t^{*}
has as objects triples
(G,E,u)
with
u:f^{*}G\rightarrow t^{*}E
in
\mathcal{F}
, with the projection
p_{1}^{*}(G,E,u)=G
. The terminal object in
f^{*}\downarrow t^{*}
is the triple
(1_{\mathcal{G}},1_{\mathcal{E}},!:f^{*}(1_{\mathcal{G}})\simeq t^{*}(1\mathcal{E}))
; a global element in
f^{*}\downarrow t^{*}
of some
(G,E,u)
is the same as a choice of
(a,b)
inscribed in a triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t^{*}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}b}

Now, a global element
a:1_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow p^{*}(G,E,u)
simply is an element
a:1_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow G
, and the composite
uf^{*}aa:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow t^{*}E
defines a global element of
t^{*}
, which lifts uniquely to a global element
b:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow E
for
t
is terminally connected. Hence, our global element for
p_{1}^{*}
lifts uniquely as this
(a,b)
in
f^{*}\downarrow t^{*}
; one can check this choice to be moreover natural in
a
.
∎

We are seeing also in section 5 that terminally connected geometric morphisms also enjoy a dual bicomma stability property, but this is not directly related to the lax orthogonality property.

4

Properties of pro-etale geometric morphisms

Pro-etale geometric morphisms have been known for some time, though not much beyond their definition; few information is available on them, and they are still lacking an intrinsic characterization. Here, we would like to provide a first systematic investigation of their properties.

4.1
Pro-adjoint aspects

We can already guess that pro-etale geometric morphisms, generalizing etaleness out of the locally connected world, shall lack the further left adjoint to the inverse image part of etale geometric morphisms, which expresses the fact that their connected components do not form anymore a set, but rather a prodiscrete space.

Proposition 4.1.1
.

A geometric morphism is etale if and only if it is pro-etale and essential.

Proof.

Suppose that
p:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is pro-etale and essential. Then
f^{*}
has a further left adjoint
f_{!}
so that the category of global elements
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}
has an initial object
\eta:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}
. But we saw at
corollary

2.2.3
that a pro-etale was always decomposable as the cofiltered bilimit over
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow p^{*}
, which by initialness reduces to the etale geometric morphism over
f_{!}(1_{\mathcal{F}})
, hence a geometric equivalence over
\mathcal{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

∎

Proposition 4.1.2
.

Any pro-etale geometric morphism is localic.

Proof.

Etale geometric morphisms are localic, corresponding to the discrete internal locales at objects. But now localic geometric morphisms are closed under cofiltered bilimits for they are a right class in an orthogonality structure.
∎

Remark 4.1.3
.

Hence, any pro-etale geometric morphism over
\mathcal{E}
defines uniquely an internal locale in
{\bf Loc}[\mathcal{E}]
, which is the cofiltered limit of the corresponding discrete internal locales - computed in
{\bf Loc}[\mathcal{E}]
. In other words, any pro-etale geometric morphism is the localic geometric morphism at a pro-discrete internal locale. Recall that the category of internal locales in
\mathcal{E}
is equivalent to the category of localic geometric morphisms over
\mathcal{E}
, and moreover that
{\bf Loc}/\mathcal{E}\hookrightarrow{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
is reflective, with the left adjoint given by the localic reflection: hence the functor sending an internal locale
L
to
{\bf Sh}_{\mathcal{E}}(L)\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
preserves limits, in particular cofiltered limits. Hence, for a pro-etale geometric morphism given by a cofiltered diagram
E:I\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, we have

\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,\mathcal{E}/E_{i}\simeq{\bf Sh}_{\mathcal{E}}(\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,E_{i})

where
\lim_{i\in I}E_{i}
is computed in the category of internal locales
{\bf Loc}[\mathcal{E}]
: any pro-etale geometric morphism is the localic morphism associated to a pro-discrete internal locale.

Remark 4.1.4
.

We saw at
corollary

2.1.13
that pro-etale geometric morphisms over
\mathcal{E}
are still discrete as objects of the 2-category
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
, in the sense that their representables return homcategories that are equivalent to sets (rather set themselves). In a sense, this definition of discreteness is a bit coarse and would rather correspond to being
groupoidal
from the point of view of the strict slice, which explains the discrepancy with the fact hat the corresponding internal locales are no more discrete.

As we see, several properties of pro-etales geometric morphisms are reformulation of properties of etale geometric morphisms in the absence of the further left adjoint to the inverse image. However, although this further left adjoint does not exist globally, it is still present in a hidden way, at the level of the
pro-completion
. There is, since
[
13
]
, a general theory of generalized adjunctions where a left adjoint only exists at the level of a free completion for some shape of diagrams. In particular is investigated the example of
right pro-adjoints
.

Definition 4.1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a category. A
pro-object
in
\mathcal{A}
is a functor
X:I\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
with
I
cofiltered. A morphism of pro-objects
(I,X)\rightarrow(J,Y)
is defined as follows; first, choose the data of a reindexing map
f:J\rightarrow I
together with a transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

which is not strictly speaking natural but natural up to the following relation: for any
d:j\rightarrow j^{\prime}
in
J
, there exists a span
u:i\rightarrow f(j)
,
u^{\prime}:i\rightarrow f(j^{\prime})
such that we have a commutation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(f(j))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(f(j^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y(j^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(u^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(d)}

Then, identify any two choices
(f,\phi)
,
(f^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
if for any
j
in
J
, there exists a span
d:i\rightarrow f(j)
,
d^{\prime}:i\rightarrow f^{\prime}(j)
and making the following diagram to commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(f(j))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(f^{\prime}(j))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(d^{\prime})}

Finally, a morphism
X\rightarrow Y
in
{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{A})
is a class for this relation. In the following, we denote as
{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{A})
the category of pro-objects and morphisms of pro-objects between them. For any locally small category
\mathcal{A}
, there is an full and faithful functor
\iota_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{A})
sending
A
on the singleton pro-object
A:*\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
.

Remark 4.1.6
.

The category
{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{A})
can also be described as the full subcategory of
[\mathcal{A},{\bf Set}]^{{\rm op}}
whose objects are those functors that are filtered colimits of corepresentables. This amounts to their category of elements being cofiltered.

Remark 4.1.7
.

If
\mathcal{A}
has a terminal object, then so does
{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{A})
, and one can check it coincides with the image
\iota_{A}(1_{\mathcal{A}})
, with the terminal map of a pro-object being given by the class of any such triangle as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{X(i)}}

Indeed one can show that any two choice of
i
in
I
are identified through the relation above.

Definition 4.1.8
.

A functor
f:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
is said to be
right pro-adjoint
if it admits a relative left adjoint
l
along the embedding
\iota_{\mathcal{A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}

which means that for any
A
in
\mathcal{A}
and
B
in
\mathcal{B}
we have a natural isomorphism

\mathcal{B}[B,f(A)]\simeq{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{A})[l(B),\iota_{\mathcal{A}}(A)]

It is known that any left exact functor is right pro-adjoint: this is a consequence that its associated nerve is a filtered colimit of representable, so that its category of elements is cofiltered. As the consequence we have the following known fact:

Proposition 4.1.9
.

Let
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
be any geometric morphism. Then
f^{*}
is right pro-adjoint.

Proof.

One can check that the relative left adjoint
f_{!}
sends
F
to the projection
\pi_{F}:F\downarrow f^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
sending
a:F\rightarrow f^{*}E
to
E
. For
f^{*}
is lex, hence representably flat, any such
F\downarrow f^{*}
is cofiltered, and this is a pro-object. Similarly it sends
u:F_{1}\rightarrow F_{2}
to (the class of) the strictly commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}\downarrow f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{2}\downarrow f^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\downarrow f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

∎

Remark 4.1.10
.

Remark that for an essential geometric morphism, each comma category
F\downarrow f^{*}
has an initial object
\eta_{F}:F\rightarrow f^{*}f_{!}F
, and the relative left adjoint actually factorizes through
\mathcal{E}
as a global left adjoint.

Some properties of etale geometric morphisms conveyed by their essential image part generalize to pro-etale geometric morphism as a property of the left pro-adjoint. For instance, recall from
[
9
]
[Proposition 6.3.5.11] that the essential image
E_{!}
of an etale morphism at any object
E
is faithful. By a manipulation of adjoints, we equivalently have that its inverse image
E^{*}
is
coseparating
, which means that two parallel arrows
a,a^{\prime}:g\rightrightarrows h
are equals in a slice topos
\mathcal{E}/E
if and only if they are jointly coequalized by all arrows of the form
h\rightarrow E_{i}^{*}E
.

Proposition 4.1.11
.

If
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is pro-etale, then the left pro-adjoint
f_{!}
is faithful.

Proof.

As
f
is pro-etale, it is up to an equivalence of the form
\mathcal{F}\simeq{\textup{bilim}}_{I}\mathcal{E}/E_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
for some cofiltered diagram
I\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, with the inverse image
f^{*}
sending an object
E
to the object represented by any triple of the form
(i,E_{i}^{*}E)
in the pseudocolimit
{\textup{pscolim}}_{I^{\rm op}}\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
. As objects of the pseudocolimit generate
\mathcal{F}
, it is sufficient to give an expression of the restriction of the left pro-adjoint
f_{!}
to the pseudocolimit, and to test faithfulness at this level. Let be a parallel pair
[(d,a)]_{\sim},[(d^{\prime},a^{\prime})]_{\sim}:[(j,g)]_{\sim}\rightrightarrows[(i,h)]_{\sim}
in
{\textup{pscolim}}_{I^{\rm op}}\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
, conssiting of a parallel pair
d,d^{\prime}:j\rightarrow i
in
I
and a span over
E_{j}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{d}^{*}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{d^{\prime}}^{*}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{d}^{*}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{d^{\prime}}^{*}D}

For
I
is cofiltered, there exists an arrow
d^{\prime\prime}:k\rightarrow j
such that
dd^{\prime\prime}=d^{\prime}d^{\prime\prime}
. Then we end up with a parallel pair over
E_{k}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{d^{\prime\prime}}^{*}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{dd^{\prime\prime}}^{*}D\simeq u_{d^{\prime}d^{\prime\prime}}^{*}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{d^{\prime\prime}}^{*}a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{d^{\prime\prime}}^{*}a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{d^{\prime\prime}}^{*}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{dd^{\prime\prime}}^{*}D\simeq u_{d^{\prime}d^{\prime\prime}}^{*}D}

such that
[(d,a)]_{\sim}=[(dd^{\prime\prime},u_{dd^{\prime\prime}}^{*}a)]_{\sim}
and
[(d^{\prime},a^{\prime})]_{\sim}=[(d^{\prime}d^{\prime\prime},u_{d^{\prime}d^{\prime\prime}}^{*}a^{\prime})]_{\sim}
. In other words, it is always possible to find representing objects such that the parallel pair is supported by a same arrow in
I
, so that it corresponds to a parallel pair in the slice over the domain object. Hence, we can assume without loss of generality that
d=d^{\prime}
, so that
u_{d}^{*}h=u_{d^{\prime}}^{*}h
in
\mathcal{E}/E_{j}
.

Suppose now that
f_{!}[(d,a)]_{\sim}=f_{!}[(d,a^{\prime})]_{\sim}
, which means that the functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(i,h)]_{\sim}\downarrow f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(j,g)]_{\sim}\downarrow f^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[(d,a)]_{\sim}\downarrow f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[(d,a^{\prime})]_{\sim}\downarrow f^{*}}

are the same; objects of
[(i,h)]_{\sim}\downarrow f^{*}
are span that can be chosen of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(l,e^{*}h)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(l,E_{l}^{*}E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,h)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{l},b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(e,1_{e^{*}D})}

Amongst them are those which arise more directly in
\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
itself from arrows
b:h\rightarrow E_{i}^{*}E
. So the condition that
[(d,a)]_{\sim}\downarrow f^{*}=[(d,a^{\prime})]_{\sim}\downarrow f^{*}
implies in particular that those two functors agree on all such elements, which means that in
\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
any composite
ba
and
ba^{\prime}
as below are equal as arrows
u_{d}g\rightarrow E_{i}^{*}E

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}\times E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{i}^{*}E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{d}}

But then this forces
a=a^{\prime}
as the objects of the form
E_{i}^{*}E
for
E
are a coseparator of
\mathcal{E}/E_{i}
. Hence,
(d,a)=(d^{\prime},a^{\prime})
, and faithfulness of the left pro-adjoint
f_{!}
. ∎

4.2
Intrinsic characterization

Let us now try to give a more intrinsic characterization of pro-etale geometric morphisms.

4.2.1
.

From the orthogonal factorization theorem, we know that a geometric morphism
f
is pro-etale if and only if its terminally connected part
t_{f}
is a geometric equivalence, which amounts to the inverse image part
t_{f}^{*}
to being an equivalence of categories, equivalently, that it is essentially surjective on objects and fully faithful.

But we saw in the proof of
theorem

3.2.1
that
t_{f}^{*}
could be computed in an explicit way. First, for an object in the pseudocolimit
{\textup{pscolim}}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\mathcal{E}/E
presented as the equivalence class of some triple
(E,a,h)
with
a:1\mathcal{F}\rightarrow f^{*}E
and
h:D\rightarrow E
, we had
t_{f}^{*}([(E,a,h)]_{\sim}=a^{*}f^{*}D
and composition of pullbacks ensures that this expression does not depend on the choice of representing objects in the equivalence class. Then, for an object
X
in the topos of sheaves over this pseudocolimit of sites, constructed as a colimit of representables
X\simeq{\textup{colim}}_{i\in I}\text{\char 136\relax}_{[(E_{i},a_{i},h_{i})]_{\sim}}
presented by a choice of family of global elements
(a_{i}:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow f^{*}E_{i}
and arrows
h_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow E_{i}
, we had the formula

t_{f}^{*}(X)={\textup{colim}}_{i\in I}a_{i}^{*}f^{*}D_{i}
.

Hence, asking for
t_{f}^{*}
to be an equivalence amounts to asking the following conditions:

-

for any object
F
in
\mathcal{F}
, there is a diagram
(E_{i},a_{i},h_{i})_{i\in I}
with
a_{i}:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}E_{i}
and
h_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow E_{i}
, with the transition morphism at a
d:i\rightarrow j
consisting in a pair
(u_{d},v_{d}):h_{i}\rightarrow h_{j}
forming a commutative square with the first component defining a morphism of elements
u_{d}:a_{i}\rightarrow a_{j}
, such that one has a colimit decomposition into the corresponding fibers:

F\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{colim}}}\;a_{i}^{*}f^{*}D_{i}

-

for any morphism
u:F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
, there is a diagram of morphisms
(u_{i},v_{i}):(E_{i},a_{i},h_{i})\rightarrow(E^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i},h^{\prime}_{i})
such that
u
is obtained as the colimit in the arrow category of the induced maps
(u_{i},v_{i}):a_{i}^{*}f^{*}D_{i}\rightarrow(a_{i}^{\prime})^{*}f^{*}D_{i}^{\prime}
;

-

moreover, the faithfulness condition says that two morphisms of diagrams that beget the same morphism between their colimit must actually be equals.

We shall say in this case that
f^{*}

generates
\mathcal{F}
under fibers of global elements
.

Proposition 4.2.2
.

A geometric morphism is pro-etale if and only if its inverse image generates its domain under fibers of global elements.

Proof.

From what precedes any pro-etale geometric morphisms generates its domain under fibers of global elements of its inverse image. Let us prove the reverse condition. Suppose that
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
satisfies this property: we must then decompose
f
as a cofiltered bilimit indexed by its diagram of global elements. In fact, we can foretell that one can choose as cofiltered indexing diagram the category of global elements
1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}
. We have to prove that
f
decomposes in
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
as the colfiltered bilimit
\lim_{1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}\pi_{E}
, or in other words, that for any other geometric morphism
g:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
one has an equivalence of homcategories

{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}[g,f]\simeq\underset{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}[g,\pi_{E}]

where the latter bilimits corresponds to families of global elements of
(b_{(E,a)}:1_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow g^{*}E)_{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}
indexed by global elements of
f^{*}
.
In one direction, it is always true that a geometric morphism
h:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
with
fh\simeq g
defines such a cone of global element
(\ulcorner a\urcorner h)_{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}
by postcomposition of
h
with the names of the corresponding element of
f^{*}
.

The converse direction makes use of the characterization. Suppose we have such a family
(b_{(E,a)}:1_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow g^{*}E)_{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}
of global elements of
g^{*}
. We want to define a geometric morphism
h:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
together with a natural transformation
g\simeq fh
such that each composite
\ulcorner a\urcorner h:g\rightarrow\pi_{E}
coincides with the name of the element
b_{(E,a)}
. Such a geometric morphism can be constructed through its inverse image as follows. As we suppose that
f
generates
\mathcal{F}
under fibers of global elements, we can choose for any
F
in
\mathcal{F}
a colimit decomposition as above in term of fibers
a_{i}^{*}f^{*}D_{i}
where
a_{i}:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}E_{i}
are a certain family of global elements of
f^{*}
and
u_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow E_{i}
a family of morphisms in
\mathcal{E}
. Each of those global elements corresponds to a global element
b_{(E_{i},a_{i})}:1_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow g^{*}E_{i}
in
\mathcal{G}
given by the cone. Then, define
h^{*}F
as the colimit of the corresponding fibers

h^{*}F\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{colim}}}\;b_{(E_{i},a_{i})}^{*}g^{*}D_{i}

with
b_{(E_{i},a_{i})}^{*}g^{*}D_{i}
being the fibers of
g^{*}(u_{i})
along
b_{(E_{i},a_{i})}
.

This construction is well defined, that is, does not depend on the choice of the colimit decomposition: suppose one has two such decompositions
F\simeq{\textup{colim}}_{i\in I}a_{i}^{*}f^{*}D_{i}\simeq{\textup{colim}}_{j\in J}b_{j}^{*}f^{*}H_{j}
with respectively
u_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow E_{i}
,
v_{j}:H_{j}\rightarrow E_{j}
the maps we take fibers of. As the cone of global elements of
g
ranges over all possible global elements of
f^{*}
both the
a_{i}
and the
b_{j}
have corresponding global elements of
g^{*}
in
\mathcal{G}
. We are going to prove the two decomposition of
F
can be refined by a common one whose image by
h^{*}
must be equal to both images. For each pair
(i,j)\in I\times J
, one can take the global element
(a_{i},b_{j}):1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}(E_{i}\times E_{j})
induced by the universal property of the product for is preserved by
f^{*}
; by composition and cancellation of pullbacks, we know that the morphism
u_{i}\times v_{j}:D_{i}\times H_{j}\rightarrow E_{i}\times E_{j}
is the pullback over
E_{i}\times E_{j}
of the pullbacks of
u_{i}
and
v_{j}
along the corresponding projections; then take the following fiber

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a_{i},b_{j})^{*}f^{*}(D_{i}\times H_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}(D_{i}\times H_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}(E_{i}\times E_{j})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a_{i},b_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(u_{i}\times v_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a_{i},b_{j})^{*}f^{*}(u_{i}\times v_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

But stability of colimits ensures that this later exactly is the fiber of the inclusions respective to the two altenrative colimit decompositions at the pair
(i,j)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a_{i},b_{j})^{*}f^{*}(D_{i}\times H_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{j}^{*}f^{*}H_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{i}^{*}f^{*}D_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{colim}}}\;a_{i}^{*}f^{*}D_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{j\in J}{{\textup{colim}}}\;b_{j}^{*}f^{*}H_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

so that one has a new colimit decomposition of
F
as a two steps colimit

F\simeq\underset{(i,j)\in I\times J}{{\textup{colim}}}\;(a_{i},b_{j})^{*}f^{*}(D_{i}\times H_{j})

But in fact, would one now fix either
i
or
j
and allow to range over the other variable, stability of colimits gives a decomposition

a_{i}^{*}f^{*}D_{i}\simeq\underset{j\in J}{{\textup{colim}}}\;(a_{i},b_{j})^{*}f^{*}(D_{i}\times H_{j})\hskip 28.45274ptb_{j}^{*}f^{*}H_{j}\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{colim}}}\;(a_{i},b_{j})^{*}f^{*}(D_{i}\times H_{j})

Now for each
(i,j)\in I\times J
both
a_{i}
and
b_{j}
correspond to global elements
\overline{a_{i}}:1_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow g^{*}E_{i}
and
\overline{b_{j}}:1_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow g^{*}E_{j}
, and so does
(a_{i},b_{i})
which defines an element
\overline{(a_{i},b_{i})}:1_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow g^{*}(E_{i}\times E_{j})
in a way that is compatible with the projections by naturality of the cone. Hence, one can take at each such pair
(i,j)
the following fiber

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{(a_{i},b_{j})}^{*}g^{*}(D_{i}\times H_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}(D_{i}\times H_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}(E_{i}\times E_{j})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(a_{i},b_{j})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(u_{i}\times v_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then commutation of colimits ensures that the value of
h^{*}
computed on both colimit decomposition must agree because of this common refinement

\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{colim}}}\;\overline{a_{i}}^{*}g^{*}D_{i}\simeq\underset{(i,j)\in I\times J}{{\textup{colim}}}\;\overline{(a_{i},b_{j})}^{*}g^{*}(D_{i}\times H_{j})\simeq\underset{j\in J}{{\textup{colim}}}\;\overline{b_{j}}^{*}g^{*}H_{j}

We let the reader convince himself of the functoriality of this construction, which relies on the universal property of colimit.
We also have a natural equivalence
h^{*}f^{*}\simeq g^{*}
, for in the case where
F
is simply of the form
f^{*}E
, the colimit decomposition condenses into a single object and one simply take
h^{*}(f^{*}E)=g^{*}E
according to our construction.

Also commutation of colimits ensures that the functor
h^{*}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
thus defined is cocontinuous. It is also lex: on one hand, it preserves the terminal object as
h^{*}(1_{\mathcal{F}})\simeq h^{*}f^{*}(1_{\mathcal{E}})\simeq g^{*}1_{\mathcal{E}}
, so it suffices to show it preserves pullbacks, but this uses a similar argument of fibers decomposition and stability of colimits. Hence, we do have a geometric morphism
h:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
which is morevoer part of a morphism
g\rightarrow f
in the pseudoslice
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
.

Also, one recovers the cone
(\overline{a})_{(E,a)\in 1_{\mathcal{F}}\downarrow f^{*}}
as
\overline{a}\simeq h^{*}(a)
by functoriality of
h^{*}
applied to each element
a:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}E
, combining the previous calculation of
h^{*}f^{*}
with the calculation at the terminal object.

Finally, let us check the uniqueness part of the universal property of the bilimit. In one sense, two cones inducing the same
h
must consist of the same global elements given by
h^{*}
. Dually suppose one has two
h,h^{\prime}
inducing the same cone, that is, agreeing on the global elements. As
\mathcal{F}
is generated under global elements, the colimit decomposition as fibers ensures that
h^{*}
and
h^{\prime*}
actually coincide on every objects. To conclude, let us observe that there is no 2-dimensional part to check in the universal property of the bilimit as etale morphisms are discrete, so that there is no morphism of cone to test the universal property at. ∎

4.3
Opfibrational aspects

Pro-etale geometric morphisms have an interesting intrinsic property inside of the 2-category of topoi, which manifests also at the level of their points: they are
discrete opfibrations
. It is easy to show that etale morphisms are opfibrations, and as we shall see below, this property is inherited by bilimits of such morphisms; moreover we saw at
corollary

2.1.13
that pro-etale geometric morphisms are discrete in the 2-dimensional sense. This suggests a relation with the
comprehensive factorization system
on
{\bf Cat}
introduced by
[
12
]
, or more exactly, is dual version (
initial functor
,
discrete opfibration
).

We already met initial functors at
definition

2.1.4
; on the other hand, the definition of discrete opfibrations in
{\bf Cat}
is rather standard; let us emphasize that, as often with right classes in
{\bf Cat}
, discrete opfibrations admit a representable analog in any 2-category:

Definition 4.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a 2-category; a morphism
f:C\rightarrow D
is an
opfibration
in
\mathcal{K}
if for any
B
in
\mathcal{K}
, the corresponding functor
\mathcal{K}[B,f]:\mathcal{K}[B,C]\rightarrow\mathcal{K}[B,D]
is an opfibration in
{\bf Cat}
. Moreover, we shall say that such a representable opfibration is
discrete
if it is a discrete object in
\mathcal{K}/D
.

Proposition 4.3.2
.

Pro-etale geometric morphisms are discrete opfibrations in
{\bf GTop}
.

Proof.

First, observe that etale geometric morphisms are representable discrete opfibrations: for any 2-cell as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

the opcartesian lift of
\phi
is provided by the morphism of global element defined as the composite
\phi^{\flat}_{E}a:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow g^{*}E
. Similarly, for a cofiltered diagram
\mathbb{E}:I\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, if one replaces in the 2-cell above the etale morphism by a pro-etale morphism, then
a
defines a cofiltered family of global elements
a_{i}:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}E_{i}
and it suffices to compose each
a_{i}
with the corresponding component
\phi^{\flat}_{E_{i}}
to have a cartesian lift – functoriality being provided by pasting each morphism of element at
d
with the corresponding naturality square of
\phi^{\flat}
at
u_{d}
. Moreover, we saw that pro-etale morphisms over
\mathcal{E}
are discrete in
{\bf GTop}/\mathcal{E}
.
∎

This property is preserved by the functor of points
{\bf pt}:{\bf GTop}\rightarrow{\bf Set}
. Recall that a point of the etale topos at
E
is the same as a pair
(x,a)
with
x
a point of
\mathcal{E}
together with
a
an element of the fiber
x^{*}E
. In fact, each object
E
defines an evaluation functor
{\bf ev}_{E}:{\bf pt}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow{\bf Set}
sending a point
x
to its fiber at
E
, which exhibits
{\bf pt}(\pi_{E})
as the discrete opfibration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf pt}(\mathcal{E}/E)\simeq\displaystyle\int{\bf ev}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf pt}(\mathcal{E})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{E}}

The functor
{\bf pt}:{\bf GTop}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
preserves bilimits for it is representable: hence for a pro-etale geometric morphism for a diagram
(\mathcal{E}/E_{i})_{i\in I}
we have

{\bf pt}(\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;\mathcal{E}/E_{i})\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\;{\bf pt}(\mathcal{E}/E_{i})

This exactly means that a point of the pro-etale topos is the same as a triples of families
((x_{i},\alpha_{d})_{i\in I,d\in I^{2}},(a_{i})_{i\in I}
with
(x_{i},\alpha_{d})_{i\in I,d\in I^{2}}
a families of points
x_{i}:{\bf Set}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
equiped with descent isomorphisms
\alpha_{d}:x_{i}\simeq x_{j}
for each
d:i\rightarrow j
in
I
satisfying the cocycles identities, together with elements of the corresponding fibers
a_{i}\in x_{i}^{*}E_{i}
satisfying the descent property that for any
d:i\rightarrow j
, with
u_{d}:E_{i}\rightarrow E_{j}
the corresponding morphism in
\mathcal{E}
, the image of
a_{i}
through either member of the following naturality square is equal to
a_{j}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{i}^{*}(E_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{i}^{*}(E_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{j}^{*}(E_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{j}^{*}(E_{j})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{i}^{*}(u_{d})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{d}_{E_{j}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{d}_{E_{i}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{j}^{*}(u_{d})}

Those observations can be summed into the following:

Proposition 4.3.3
.

The functor
{\bf pt}
sends pro-etale geometric morphisms to discrete opfibrations.

Remark 4.3.4
.

Beware here that we use the relaxed notion of discrete opfibration where we require fibers to be actually
equivalent
to discrete categories than discrete themselves. In fact, as the points
x_{i}
in such a coherent family as above are all isomorphic, there is no distinguished choice of a point, only of the descent isomorphisms. Hence, the fibers of a point of
\mathcal{E}
does not just contains a discrete family of elements of its fibers at the
E_{i}
, but actually form a groupoid whose connected components are coherent families of points isomorphic to it, together with elements of their fibers. This is because we allowed pro-etale geometric morphisms to be
bilimits
of etale morphisms, which in
{\bf Cat}
begets pseudolimits, rather than strict limits.

5

Properties of terminally connected geometric morphisms

Dually, we should give a few additional properties about terminally connected geometric morphisms. In this sections, we first give miscelaneous properties and characterizations, either from the point of view of the inverse image or its left pro-adjoint. After some site theoretic criteria, we turn to their stability properties, in particular to special laxness properties completing those established in
section

3.3
, and conlude one some conjectures about their relation with initial functors.

5.1
General properties

Terminally connected geometric morphisms are those which are connected from the point of view of the terminal object; hence, whenever the terminal object is generating, this is enough to ensure connectedness: as
{\bf Set}
precisely is the only topos having this property, this means that there is no distinction between connected topos and terminally connected topos:

Proposition 5.1.1
.

For any Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
, the terminal geometric morphisms
\gamma_{\mathcal{E}}:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf Set}
is terminally connected if and only if it is connected.

Proof.

\gamma_{\mathcal{E}}
is terminally connected if one has a natural isomorphism
\mathcal{E}[1_{\mathcal{E}},\gamma_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}]\simeq{\bf Set}[1,-]
but for
1
is generating in
{\bf Set}
, the later is the identity functor of
{\bf Set}
, while
\mathcal{E}[1_{\mathcal{E}},\gamma_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}]=\Gamma_{\mathcal{E}}\gamma_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}
: hence the natural isomorphism witnessed by terminal connectedness provides a natural isomorphism
\Gamma_{\mathcal{E}}\gamma_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}\simeq{\rm id}_{\bf Set}
which is an inverse of the unit of the
\gamma_{\mathcal{E}}\dashv\Gamma_{\mathcal{E}}
adjunction: this forces
\gamma^{*}_{\mathcal{E}}
to be fully faithful, hence
\mathcal{E}
to be connected.
∎

It is known that a locally connected morphism
f
is connected if and only if the global left adjoint
f_{!}
preserves the terminal object. However, in the case of an essential geometric morphism, this condition only amounts to being a terminally connected morphism, and indeed, the latter were originally defined as such in
[
2
]
[4.7]. In absence of the global left adjoint, we can still nevertheless say the following:

Proposition 5.1.2
.

Let be
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
a geometric morphism. Then
f
is terminally connected iff
f_{!}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{E})
preserves the terminal object.

Proof.

Recall from
definition

4.1.5
that there is a fully faithful embedding
\iota_{\mathcal{E}}:\mathcal{E}\hookrightarrow{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{E})
, which sends in particular
1_{\mathcal{E}}
to the terminal pro-object as stated in
remark

4.1.7
. For any object
E
in
\mathcal{E}
we hence have an equivalence

{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{E})[1_{{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{E})},\iota_{\mathcal{E}}(E)]\simeq\mathcal{E}[1_{\mathcal{E}},E]

Hence, terminal connectedness of
f
whenever
f_{!}(1_{\mathcal{F}})=1_{{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{E})}
as then

\mathcal{F}[1_{\mathcal{F}},f^{*}E]\simeq{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{E})[1_{{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{E})},\iota_{\mathcal{E}}(E)]

Conversely, if
f
is terminally connected, then
f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}
and
1_{{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{E})}
are identified left to any object of the form
\iota_{E}
. But
{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{E})
is cogenerated from
\mathcal{E}
by cofiltered limits: hence we must have
f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}\simeq 1_{{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{E})}

∎

We can also give a dual of
proposition

4.2.2
: as well as pro-etale geometric morphisms are those whose codomain is generated under colimits from fibers of global elements, terminally connected are those whose inverse image is closed under such colimits of fibers:

Proposition 5.1.3
.

A geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is terminally connected if and only if any any colimit of fibers is actually in the image of
f^{*}
, and any arrow induced by such colimits from a morphism of diagram with transition morphism in the image of
t_{f}^{*}
is also in the image of
f^{*}
.

Proof.

In one direction, take a global element
a:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}E
: t
Let
F
decompose as a colimit
F\simeq{\textup{colim}}_{i\in I}a_{i}^{*}f^{*}D_{i}
with
h_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow E_{i}
in
\mathcal{E}
. Then if
f
is terminally connected, the
a_{i}
arise uniquely from global elements
\overline{a_{i}}
in
\mathcal{E}
, and we could then compute already the colimit of the corresponding fibers in
\mathcal{E}
before applying
f^{*}
for it preserves both colimits and pullbacks:

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{colim}}}\;a_{i}^{*}f^{*}D_{i}

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{colim}}}\;f^{*}\overline{a_{i}}^{*}D_{i}

\displaystyle\simeq f^{*}\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{colim}}}\;\overline{a_{i}}^{*}D_{i}

Hence,
F
lies in the essential image of
f^{*}
.
∎

Some properties of connected morphisms relative to coproducts and cardinals partially generalize. First, recall that
f
being connected amounts for all the units
\eta_{E}
to being a natural isomorphisms; in terminal connectedness, this is only “globally true” in the sense that
\Gamma_{\mathcal{E}}
naturally inverts the unit
\eta
, which can be rephrased as:

Proposition 5.1.4
.

A geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is terminally connected iff
1_{\mathcal{E}}
is
\eta
-orthogonal.

Proof.

Any
a:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow f_{*}f^{*}E
corresponds to a unique
\overline{a}:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}E
with
a=\eta_{E}f_{*}(a)
, but if one can lift uniquely
\overline{a}
to some
b:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow E
then automatically from
remark

1.3.3
we are done. Conversely, if one has orthogonality, for any
a:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}E
the induced
f_{*}(a)
lifts uniquely along
\eta_{E}
and provides the desired lift.
∎

Remark 5.1.5
.

Another easy observation is that, though a terminally connected does not necessarily lift generalized elements in general, it lifts at least those indexed by locally constant sheaves (those of that are
{\bf Set}
-indexed coproducts of the terminal objects): for
X=\coprod_{i\in I}1_{\mathcal{F}}
: then one has
[X,f^{*}]\simeq[\coprod_{i\in I}1_{\mathcal{E}},-]
. In other words, one can lift uniquely
I
-indexed families of global elements of a same object, and in a natural way.

In particular, terminally connected morphisms fit in a pattern of connectedness involving full faithfulness of extensions or restrictions of the inverse image part relative to different classes of internal locales, from the more general to the more discrete:

Proposition 5.1.6
.

For a geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
:

-

f
is hyperconnected iff
f^{*}:{\bf Frm}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow{\bf Frm}(\mathcal{F})
is fully faithful

-

f
is connected iff
f^{*}:{\mathbf{disc}}{\bf Frm}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow{\mathbf{disc}}{\bf Frm}(\mathcal{F})
is fully faithful

-

if
f
is terminally connected then
f^{*}:{\mathbf{Card}}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow{\mathbf{Card}}(\mathcal{F})
is fully faithful;

-

if
f
is pure then
f^{*}:{\mathbf{fin}}{\mathbf{Card}}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow{\mathbf{fin}}{\mathbf{Card}}(\mathcal{F})
is fully faithful.

Proof.

The first item is
[
6
]
[Proposition C2.4.15]; the second is immediate as discrete frames are exactly objects of the topos. For the third item, we saw at
remark

5.1.5
that terminally connected geometric morphisms lift uniquely set-indexed families of global elements, which applies in particular to cardinals: if
\alpha
and
\beta
are two cardinals, with corresponding cardinals in
\mathcal{E}
being given as the coproduct
\coprod_{\alpha}1_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\coprod_{\beta}1_{\mathcal{E}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[f^{*}\coprod_{\alpha}1_{\mathcal{E}},f^{*}\coprod_{\beta}1_{\mathcal{E}}]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{F}[\coprod_{\alpha}1_{\mathcal{F}},f^{*}\coprod_{\beta}1_{\mathcal{E}}]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{E}[\coprod_{\alpha}1_{\mathcal{E}},\coprod_{\beta}1_{\mathcal{E}}]

The same idea applies to the pure case, but for finite cardinals.
∎

We also have a dual property, which states that the direct image
f_{*}
of a terminally connected morphism “globally” preserves cardinals:

Proposition 5.1.7
.

If
f
is terminally connected then for any
\alpha
we have
\Gamma_{\mathcal{E}}f_{*}\coprod_{\alpha}1_{\mathcal{F}}\simeq\Gamma_{\mathcal{E}}\coprod_{\alpha}1_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof.

This simply relies on the natural isomorphism
\Gamma_{\mathcal{F}}f^{*}\simeq\Gamma_{\mathcal{E}}
.
∎

5.2
Site-theoretic aspects

Let us now give a few site-theoretic criteria to test terminal connectedness. We first discuss corresponding properties for morphisms of sites, and then for comorphisms of sites.

Remark 5.2.1
.

We should first emphasize that things are not as well behaved as one might think. Given a morphism of site
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
with terminal object, one could restrict the lifting condition of global elements to those ones arising from those sites: that is, that any global element
1_{\mathcal{D}}\rightarrow f^{*}C
should come from a unique global element
1_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow C
in
\mathcal{C}
in a natural way. This rises a double warning:

-

asking
f
to lift uniquely global elements may not be sufficient in general to ensure that the associated geometric morphism
{\bf Sh}(f):{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)
is terminally connected. Indeed, although the sites generate the associated topoi through colimit, it may happen that the terminal object is too “coarse” in the topos to retrieve global elements of arbitrary objects constructed as colimits of representables. Indeed, take a global element
a:1_{\mathcal{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}}\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}(F)
with
F={\textup{colim}}_{i\in I}\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}_{C_{i}}
its colimit decomposition: even though
{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}({\textup{colim}}_{i\in I}\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}_{C_{i}})\simeq{\textup{colim}}_{i\in I}\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(C_{i})}
, using lifting of elements by
f
would first require to lift
a
through some member of this colimit, which is not possible in all generality. We saw at
lemma

3.1.2
that it is possible to find, for a given geometric morphism, suitable small sites on which testing terminal connectedness was possible, but this involved special sites where representable generated objects under filtered colimits for some cardinal against which the terminal object was compact.

-

conversely, terminal connectedness does not imply always the possibility to lift global elements inside of the given site: if one has
a:1_{\mathcal{D}}\rightarrow f(C)
in
\mathcal{D}
, then one can lift uniquely the corresponding element
\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}_{a}:1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}
to some
b:1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}\rightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}
in
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)
, but in absence of subcanonicity, such a global element may not arise from the site. However, this is exactly the only hindrance to do so:

Proposition 5.2.2
.

Let
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
be a morphism of site between site with terminal object such that
{\bf Sh}(f)
is terminally connected. If both
(\mathcal{C},J)
and
(\mathcal{D},K)
are subcanonical, then
f
lifts uniquely global elements from
\mathcal{D}
to
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

In the case where both sites are subcanonical, both
\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}
are fully faithful, and assuming
{\bf Sh}(f)
to be terminally connected yields the following isomorphism in each
C
of
\mathcal{C}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[1_{\mathcal{D}},f(C)]

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)[1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)},{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{C})]

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)[1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)},\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{C}[1_{\mathcal{C}},C]

∎

For the converse problem of deducing terminal connectedness from lifting of global elements from a site, we know at least two cases where it holds. The first one is cardinal-sensitive, and is actually close in mind of the argument we used at
lemma

3.1.2
:

Lemma 5.2.3
.

Let
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
be a geometric morphism; if one has a cardinal
\mu
and a small standard site
(\mathcal{C},J)
for
\mathcal{E}
such that
\mathcal{C}
generates
\mathcal{E}
under
\mu
-filtered colimits and both
1_{\mathcal{F}}
and
1_{\mathcal{E}}
are
\mu
-compact, then
f^{*}
is terminally connected if and only if one has a natural isomorphism
\mathcal{F}[1_{\mathcal{F}},f^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}]\simeq\mathcal{C}[1_{\mathcal{C}},-]
.

Proof.

If one has a site
(\mathcal{C},J)
with all the conditions above, then one has a sequence of natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[1_{\mathcal{F}},f^{*}F]

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{\mathcal{C}\downarrow F}{{\textup{colim}}}\,\mathcal{F}[1_{\mathcal{F}},f^{*}C]

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{\mathcal{C}\downarrow F}{{\textup{colim}}}\,\mathcal{E}[1_{\mathcal{E}},C]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{E}[1_{\mathcal{E}},F]

∎

Remark 5.2.4
.

Beware that here the condition that
1_{\mathcal{E}}
is also
\mu
-compact is crucial. However, we can always choose the site
(C,J)
in such a way that this additional condition is satisfied. Take directly
\mu
sufficiently large to ensure that
\mathcal{E}
is locally
\mu
-presentable and both
1_{\mathcal{E}},1_{\mathcal{F}}
are
\mu
-compact, and then take the closure of
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
under finite limits in
\mathcal{E}
.

Even better is the case where the terminal object is infinitesimal - for accessibility conditions are no longer required. Recall that a site
(\mathcal{D},K)
is said to be
local
if the terminal object is
K
-indecomposable – that is, the only covering sieve for
1_{\mathcal{D}}
is the maximal sieve, see
[
6
]
[C3.6.3 (d)]. Then for such a site,
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)
is a local topos.

Lemma 5.2.5
.

Let
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
be a morphism of sites between small lex sites with
(\mathcal{D},K)
a local site. If
f
lifts global elements then
{\bf Sh}(f):{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},J)\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)
is terminally connected. Moreover, if
(\mathcal{C},J)
and
(\mathcal{D},K)
both are subcanonical, the converse also holds.

Proof.

Suppose that
f
lifts global elements, so that we have a natural isomorphism
\mathcal{D}[1_{\mathcal{D}},f]\simeq\mathcal{C}[1_{\mathcal{C}},-]
. We prove this suffices to lift also global elements of
{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}
that arise in
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)
. Let be
a:1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}E
with
E
a object of
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)
. Since
\mathcal{C}
generates
\mathcal{E}
under colimits and
1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}
is tiny by localness of
{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}
, there exists a factorization of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}(x)}

which is moreover essentially unique in the sense that for any two such lifts are identified with
a
in the colimit
{\textup{colim}}_{(C,a)\in\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}\downarrow E}{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)[1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)},{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}]
.

As
{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}
restricts as
f
from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
, one has
{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(C)}

It suffices then to prove one can lift uniquely global elements of the form
b:1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}\rightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(C)}
. Beware that such maps do not necessarily come from
\mathcal{D}
for the topology
K
is not supposed to be subcanonical so
\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}
may not be fully faithful. However, since
K
covers are sent to canonical cover in
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)
, and the terminal object comes from the terminal object of the site
1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{1_{\mathcal{D}}}
, we know that any such global element
b
is induced through the universal property of colimits by the image of a
K
-cover
(u_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow 1_{\mathcal{D}})_{i\in I}
together with a cocone
(v_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow f(C))_{i\in I}
such that for any
i
in
I
one has

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{D_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(C)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{u_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{v_{i}}}

But by localness of the site (equivalently, by localness of
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)
), the identity of
1_{\mathcal{D}}
factorizes through some
u_{i}
as
1_{\mathcal{D}}=u_{i}w
in
\mathcal{D}
. This factorization is essentially unique in the sense that for any other factorization through
1_{\mathcal{D}}=u_{i^{\prime}}w^{\prime}
then
w,w^{\prime}
factorize through the pullback
D_{i}\times_{D}D_{i^{\prime}}
and are hence identified in the corresponding colimit in
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)
. Hence, we get an element
v_{i}w:1_{\mathcal{D}}\rightarrow f(C)
in
\mathcal{D}
, which comes uniquely from an element
\overline{c}:1_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow C
in
\mathcal{C}
. For
w
is a section of
u_{i}
, we have that
f(c)=b
.
∎

One may ask dually what kind of
comorphisms of sites
induce terminally connected geometric morphisms: this question was answered for the case of essential terminally connected geometric in
[
2
]
in term of an notion of
cofinality relative to a Grothendieck topology
.

Definition 5.2.6
.

A functor
f:A\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is
J
-cofinal
relative to a Grothendieck topology
J
on
\mathcal{C}
if it satisfies the following two conditions:

-

for any
c
in
\mathcal{C}
, there is a
J
-cover
(u_{i}:C_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
and for each
i\in I
an object
a_{i}
of
A
and an arrow
v_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow f(a_{i})
;

-

for any span
u:c\rightarrow f(a)
,
u^{\prime}:c\rightarrow f(a^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{C}
there is a
J
-cover
(u_{i}:C_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
such that for each
i\in I
the composites
uu_{i}
and
u^{\prime}u_{i}
belong to the same connected component of
c_{i}\downarrow f
.

Proposition 5.2.7

(
[
2
]
[Proposition 4.64])
.

Let
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
be a continuous comorphism of small generated sites. Then the induced geometric morphism
C_{f}:{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)
is terminally connected if and only if
f
is
K
-cofinal.

Remark 5.2.8
.

This result characterizes comorphism presentations of
essential
terminally connected geometric morphisms, as continuous comorphisms are those which induce an essential geometric morphism, see
[
2
]
[theorem 4.20].

5.3
Stability properties

Now, we turn to stability properties of terminally connected geometric morphism. Although terminally connected geometric morphisms are not expected to be stable under pullback, there is another kind of stability they do satisfy, which is also commonly encountered amongst other classes of geometric morphisms. We say that a squares in
{\bf GTop}
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

satisfies the
(right) Beck-Chevalley condition
if the canonical transformation
t^{*}f_{*}\rightarrow q_{*}p^{*}
is a natural isomorphism.

Lemma 5.3.1
.

Let be a square as above satisfying the Beck-Chevalley condition; then
p
is terminally connected whenever
t
is.

Proof.

Take a global element
a:1_{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow p^{*}G
: for
1_{\mathcal{H}}\simeq q^{*}1_{\mathcal{F}}
, this global element corresponds uniquely by adjunction to a global element
a^{\prime}:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow q_{*}p^{*}G
, but the later is canonically isomorphic to
t^{*}f_{*}G
: for
t
is terminally connected,
a^{\prime}
lifts uniquely to a global element
a^{\prime\prime}:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow f_{*}G
, whence again throuhg adjunction a global element
a^{\prime\prime\prime}:f^{*}1_{\mathcal{E}}\simeq 1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow G
.
∎

From this observation, we can improve a bit the stability condition along pullbacks. There is an important class of geometric morphisms known as
tidy
, those
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
whose direct image
f_{*}
preserves filtered
\mathcal{E}
-indexed colimits. We choose not to say more about this definition – an explanation of which can be found at
[
6
]
[Definition C3.4.2] ; let us rather recall the following property, which is
[
6
]
[Theorem 3.4.7]: take a square of geometric morphisms as above, such that
f
is tidy ; if the square is a pullback square, then not only
q
is also tidy, but then the square satisfies the Beck-Chevalley condition. This property, conjointly with the previous lemma, immediately yields the following:

Proposition 5.3.2
.

Terminally connected geometric morphisms are stable under pullback along tidy geometric morphisms.

This is not the only stability property of terminally connected geometric morphisms. Not only, as we saw at
proposition

3.3.3
, terminally connected are stable left to bicocomma squares, a property that is related to the lax orthogonality of the comprehensive factorization of geometric morphism, but in fact, they also enjoy a dual property relative to bicomma squares:

Proposition 5.3.3
.

Terminally connected geometric morphisms are stable left to bicomma squares: that is, in a bicomma square as below in
{\bf GTop}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t\downarrow f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{t,f}}

then
p_{1}
is terminally connected whenever
t
is.

Proof.

The bicomma can be presented as the sheaf topos over the small generated site obtained as the bicocomma in
{\bf Lex}
of the canonical site (where
J_{t,f}
is the topology induced by bicocomma inclusions):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{E},J_{\rm can})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F},J_{\rm can})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{G},J_{\rm can})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(t^{*}\uparrow f^{*},J_{t,f})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{t^{*},f^{*}}}

By a pseudomonadicity argument, this bicolimit in
{\bf Lex}
is actually the free cartesian category over the cocomma
t^{*}\uparrow f^{*}
computed in
{\bf Cat}
; but the latter is the category defined as follows:

-

its objects are the disjoint union of the objects of
\mathcal{F}
and the objects of
\mathcal{G}

-

it has as arrows the arrows of
\mathcal{F}
between objects of
\mathcal{F}
, the arrows of
\mathcal{G}
between the objects of
\mathcal{G}
, and for each object of
\mathcal{E}
, one has a formal arrow
\psi_{E}:t^{*}(E)\rightarrow f^{*}E
;

-

then one adds all composites of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

-

then one quotients by enforcing the composites
(\psi_{E}{\rm id}_{t^{*}E}
and
{\rm id}_{f^{*}E}\psi_{E}
to be both equal to
\psi_{E}
, and identifying any two composites
(a,E,b)
and
(a^{\prime},E^{\prime},b^{\prime})
that are related by a zigzag.

At this point, the component of
\psi
at the terminal object
1_{\mathcal{E}}
cannot be enforced to be an isomorphism. However, it becomes terminal in the free lex completion (which is the bicocomma in the 2-category of lex categories), as the bicocomma inclusions have to preserve limits and in particular the terminal object on which they must agree: hence
\psi_{1_{\mathcal{E}}}
can be taken as the identity of the terminal object in
t^{*}\uparrow f^{*}
.

Then we prove that the cocomma inclusion
q_{1}
lifts global elements. This inclusion simply sends
G
to the object
G
. From the previous consideration on the terminal object in the bicocomma, we know that global elements of
q_{1}
in the bicocomma may arise in two ways: either they are of the form
b:1_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow G
, but as those come from
\mathcal{G}
they are trivially lifted; either they arise as composite of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

But in this case, for
t
is terminally connected, there exists a unique global element
\overline{a}:1_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow E
with
a=t^{*}a
in
\mathcal{E}
, but now the naturality of
\psi
at
\overline{a}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\mathcal{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\overline{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1_{\mathcal{E}}}}

ensures that the composite
bf^{*}a:1_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow G
is a convenient lift for the global element above.
Hence, we proved
q_{1}
to lift uniquely global elements; but this is the inverse image part of the bicomma projection
p_{1}
, which is hence terminally connected.
∎

Remark 5.3.4
.

This latter result leads us to the concluding remark of this work and a conjecture about the relation between our factorization system and the comprehensive factorization. In general, 2-dimensional factorization systems with comma-stable left classes are not that common: the only other instance we know in
{\bf Cat}
is precisely the comprehensive factorization system (initial, discrete opfibration) ! Bicomma stability of terminally connected geometric morphisms remarkably mirrors the fact, first observed in lemma 0.6 of
[
10
]
, that initial functors are themselves left stable under comma squares: that is, for a comma square as below in
{\bf Cat}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i\downarrow f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{i,f}}

then
p_{1}
is initial whenever
i
is. It is not difficult to convince oneself of this fact: recall indeed that an object of the comma is a triple
(c,b,u:i(c)\rightarrow f(b))
; if
i
is initial, then for any
b
in
B
, the category
i\downarrow f(b)
is non empty and connected. Hence, there is
u:i(c)\rightarrow f(b)
in
D
from which
p_{1}(c,b,u)=b
ensures that
p_{1}\downarrow b
is non empty (and even surjective on objects). Similarly, for
i
is initial, any two such
(c,b,u)
and
(c^{\prime},b,u^{\prime})
on the same
b
are related by a zigzag in
C
. Hence,
p_{1}
is initial.

Remark 5.3.5
.

At first sight, this suggests that terminally connected morphisms are initial in some internal sense. However it is not clear which version of initialness would be suited – the reader may convince himself that the different possible notion of representable initialness are not very relevant. Left classes in factorization systems are indeed often more difficult to characterize.

Moreover, whatever initialness property they would fulfill, it would not be properly conveyed to ordinary initialness by the functor of points. We saw that pro-etale morphisms are sent to discrete opfibrations. However, it is not true that terminally connected morphisms induce initial functors between categories of points: take for instance the case of an essential geometric morphism
f
and its terminally connected, etale factorization through
\mathcal{E}/f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}
corresponding to the unit
\eta^{!}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}}:1_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow f^{*}f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}
seen as a global element. A point of
\mathcal{E}/f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}
is a pair
(x,a)
with
x
a point of
\mathcal{E}
and
a\in x^{*}(f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}})
an element of the fiber of
x^{*}
at
f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}
. The functor
{\bf pt}(t_{f})
, where
t_{f}
is the terminally connected part of the factorization, sends a point
y
of
\mathcal{F}
to the pair
({\bf pt}(f)(y),y^{*}(\eta^{!}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}}))
, where
\eta^{!}_{1_{\mathcal{F}}}:y^{*}1_{\mathcal{F}}\simeq 1_{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow y^{*}f^{*}f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}
is the image of the unit along
y^{*}
. As
{\bf pt}(\pi_{f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}}})
is a discrete fibration, all morphisms in
{\bf pt}(\mathcal{E}/f_{!}1_{\mathcal{F}})
are cartesian lifts of underlying morphisms between points in
{\bf pt}(\mathcal{E})
. Hence, if a point
x
of
\mathcal{E}
has empty comma
{\bf pt}(f)\downarrow x
, then so is
{\bf pt}(t_{f})\downarrow x
, and the induced functor
{\bf pt}(t_{f})
needs not be initial, though
t_{f}
is terminally connected.

Remark 5.3.6
.

As disappointing as the previous remark may sound, we nevertheless conjecture a subtler relation between those two classes. We saw at
proposition

5.3.3
that terminally connected geometric morphisms are stable left to bicomma squares in
{\bf GTop}
. Now if
f
is terminally connected, for terminally connected are stable left to bicomma squares by
proposition

5.3.3
, the bicomma topos
f\downarrow x
at any point
x:{\bf Set}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is terminally connected over
{\bf Set}
, hence a connected topos by
proposition

5.1.1
. For this is true at any point, this means in some sense that terminally connected geometric morphisms are “topologically initial” in the sense that their initialness, though not witnessed by points nor in an internal way, is still manifest as a topological property of the bicomma object. We hope such a statement will be made more precise in a future work relying on a convenient notion of “topological internalization”.
